Minimum transformation representations of diagram monoids


Abstract
We obtain formulae for the minimum transformation degrees of the most well-studied families of finite diagram monoids, including the partition, Brauer, Temperley–Lieb and Motzkin monoids. For example, the partition monoid 𝒫 n \mathcal{P}_{n} has degree 1 + B ⁡ ( n + 2 ) − B ⁡ ( n + 1 ) + B ⁡ ( n ) 2 1+\frac{B(n+2)-B(n+1)+B(n)}{2} for n ≥ 2 n\geq 2 , where these are Bell numbers. The proofs involve constructing explicit faithful representations of the minimum degree, many of which can be realised as (partial) actions on projections. Keywords : Diagram monoids, transformation representations, transformation degrees. MSC: 20M20, 20M30, 05E16.

1
Introduction

The study of permutation groups is as old as group theory itself, going back to the work of Galois on polynomials. In a sense, group theory
is
the study of permutation groups; indeed, as soon as Cayley gave the axioms of a group, he showed that every abstract group
G
embeds in the symmetric group
\mathcal{S}_{G}
. In the standard embedding, each
g\in G
is mapped to the right translation
\rho_{g}:G\to G
, acting by
a\rho_{g}=ag
for all
a\in G
. Thus, when
G
is finite, it embeds in a finite symmetric group
\mathcal{S}_{n}
. The smallest such
n
is called the
(minimum permutation) degree
of
G
. This invariant is notoriously difficult to calculate, even for fairly simple groups, and can interact unintuitively with basic constructions such as (semi)direct products and quotients; see for example
[
18
,
17
,
24
,
31
,
34
,
39
,
57
,
69
,
58
,
38
,
8
,
53
]
.

The proof of Cayley’s Theorem works without modification to show that any finite monoid
M
(and hence any finite semigroup) embeds in a finite full transformation monoid
\mathcal{T}_{n}
, which consists of all self-maps of
\{1,\ldots,n\}
. The smallest such
n
is called the
(minimum transformation) degree
of
M
, and is denoted
\deg(M)
.
Much of the initial work on transformation degrees was undertaken by Easdown and Schein
[
14
,
15
,
16
,
59
,
60
]
; for some earlier work on transformation representations see
[
65
,
67
,
61
,
29
]
. See also
[
6
,
20
,
19
,
30
,
3
,
43
,
42
,
40
,
25
]
for connections to computational algebra, and
[
10
,
49
,
50
,
64
,
2
,
63
,
62
,
44
,
55
]
for related studies on linear representations of semigroups and monoids.

It can again be difficult to calculate the degree of a given semigroup, even in very simple cases, and there are very few papers in the literature on this topic. As illustrative examples, fix an integer
p\geq 1
, and let
L_{p}
and
R_{p}
be
left-
and
right-zero semigroups
of size
p
, whose products obey the respective rules
xy=x
or
xy=y
. Easdown showed in
[
16
]
(see also
[
27
]
) that

\displaystyle\deg(L_{p})

\displaystyle=\min\{{n\geq 1}:{r^{n-r}\geq p\text{ for some }1\leq r\leq n}\},

\displaystyle\text{and}\hskip 20.00003pt\deg(R_{p})

\displaystyle=\min\{{n\geq 1}:{\textstyle{\prod}\lambda\geq p\text{ for some integer partition $\lambda\vdash n$}}\},

and posed the problem of calculating the degree of an arbitrary
rectangular band

L_{p}\times R_{q}
. This problem was open for more than thirty years, and was eventually solved in
[
9
]
, where it was translated to a question concerning proper colourings of uniform hypergraphs, leading to:

\deg(L_{p}\times R_{q})=\min_{r\geq 2}\beta_{r}(p,q),\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003pt\beta_{r}(p,q)={\min}\big\{{n}:{\pi_{r}(n-\lceil\log_{r}p\rceil)\geq q}\big\}.

Here
\pi_{r}(m)
is the maximum product
\prod\lambda
of a partition
\lambda\vdash m
with
r
parts.

The current article concerns the class of
diagram monoids
, which consist of various kinds of set partitions that are depicted and multiplied diagrammatically. These monoids, and related algebras and categories built from them, have origins and applications in many fields of mathematics and science. See the seminal studies
[
7
,
5
,
35
,
45
,
66
,
47
]
, and the surveys
[
46
,
37
]
; more background, and many more references, can be found in the introduction to
[
21
]
.

The diagram monoids we consider here are all submonoids of the partition monoid
\mathcal{P}_{n}
. This monoid, which will be defined below, contains a copy of the full transformation monoid
\mathcal{T}_{n}
, as well as its
opposite monoid

\mathcal{T}_{n}^{\operatorname{op}}
. Margolis and Steinberg have recently shown
[
44
]
that
\deg(\mathcal{T}_{n}^{\operatorname{op}})=2^{n}
, and this leads to the lower bound of
\deg(\mathcal{P}_{n})\geq 2^{n}
. As far as we are aware, no upper bound for
\deg(\mathcal{P}_{n})
has been given in the literature, apart from
\deg(\mathcal{P}_{n})\leq|\mathcal{P}_{n}|
, which comes directly from Cayley’s Theorem.
However, since
\mathcal{P}_{n}
is fundamental
[
23
]
, it follows from
[
33
, Theorem 5]
that
\mathcal{P}_{n}
can be faithfully represented in
\mathcal{T}_{P}\times\mathcal{T}_{P}^{\operatorname{op}}
, where
P=P(\mathcal{P}_{n})
is the set of
projections
of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
(again, see below for the precise definitions). Combining this with the result from
[
44
]
mentioned above, this leads to the upper bound of
\deg(\mathcal{P}_{n})\leq|P|+2^{|P|}
. For example, taking
n=3
, and combining this last inequality with
\deg(\mathcal{P}_{n})\geq 2^{n}
yields:

8\leq\deg(\mathcal{P}_{3})\leq 4194\ 326.

This upper bound is not an improvement on Cayley’s Theorem, which gives
{\deg(\mathcal{P}_{3})\leq|\mathcal{P}_{3}|=203}
. As we will see, the exact value of
\deg(\mathcal{P}_{3})
turns out to be
22
, which is a special case of a general formula we establish. Specifically, we will show that

\deg(\mathcal{P}_{n})=1+\frac{B(n+2)-B(n+1)+B(n)}{2},

(1.1)

where here
B(n)
is the
n
th Bell number. The ‘
1+
’ in the above formula can be removed to obtain the minimum
partial
transformation degree, which we denote by
\deg^{\prime}(\mathcal{P}_{n})
.

We also obtain analogous results for several other important families of diagram monoids—namely the Brauer monoid
\mathcal{B}_{n}
, the partial Brauer monoid
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{n}
, the planar partition monoid
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}
, the Motzkin monoid
\mathcal{M}_{n}
, and the Temperley–Lieb monoid
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
—in terms of equally-famous number sequences, such as Catalan and Motzkin numbers.
These formulae (stated for partial transformation degrees) are summarised in Table
1
; calculated values are given in Table
2
.

The process of discovering the above formula for
\deg(\mathcal{P}_{n})
was somewhat circuitous, and involved several distinct steps. Due to the lack of general theoretical techniques for calculating minimal degrees,
4
4

4

The aforementioned paper of Margolis and Steinberg
[
44
]
contains some very general results on minimal partial transformation representations of
Rhodes semisimple
semigroups, i.e. finite semigroups possessing faithful semisimple matrix representations. As noted in
[
23
]
, the minimal ideals of our diagram monoids are non-trivial square bands, and for reasons explained in
[
44
]
this is an obstruction to Rhodes-semisimplicity. Consequently, the results of
[
44
]
cannot be applied here, and it was necessary to develop tools for moving beyond the semisimple case.
we began with some computational experiments using the Semigroups package for GAP
[
26
,
48
]
, as well as some recent advances described in
[
3
, Section 5.7]
. It has long been known
[
67
]
that any finite monoid
M
acts on the quotient
M/\sigma
for any right congruence
\sigma
. The minimum degree of a
faithful
‘right congruence action’ is denoted
\deg_{\operatorname{rc}}(M)
, and this is an upper bound for
\deg(M)
. Moreover, the action of
M
on
M/\sigma
is faithful precisely when
\sigma
contains no non-trivial two-sided congruence, and for any such faithful right congruence
\sigma
we have

\deg(M)\leq\deg_{\operatorname{rc}}(M)\leq|M/\sigma|.

The algorithms described in
[
3
]
allowed us to compute minimal faithful right congruences of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
for
{n\leq 3}
, and obtain upper bounds for
\deg_{\operatorname{rc}}(\mathcal{P}_{n})
for
4\leq n\leq 7
. It was not easy for us to comprehend the minimal congruences initially, but eventually we were able to understand a near-minimal one, and this led us to discover a general construction that will be outlined in Section
3.4
. In the case of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
, the congruence had two kinds of classes: one was a right ideal, and the others were all pieces of Green’s
\mathscr{L}
-classes. These are in turn indexed by the set
P
of projections, which are special idempotents that are fixed by the involution of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
. Investigating the movement of these projections, rather than that of the
\mathscr{L}
-classes themselves, led us to the key idea of
partial actions
of so-called
regular
*
-semigroups

[
52
]
on their projections; see Section
3.5
. We were able to show that the partial action of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
on
P
is faithful, using the classification of two-sided congruences in
[
23
]
. The nature of the proof of this fact in turn led us to find a faithful sub-act
Q\subseteq P
, consisting of projections of suitably-low ‘rank’, yielding the upper bound
\deg_{\operatorname{rc}}(\mathcal{P}_{n})\leq 1+|Q|
. A combinatorial argument then shows that this reduces to the right-hand side of (
1.1
).
Showing that
1+|Q|
is also a
lower
bound for
\deg(\mathcal{P}_{n})
required an entirely different set of new techniques. These are necessarily technical in nature, so we defer their further discussion until Section
4.2
, where we will first use them. It is worth noting, however, that the methods discussed so far apply uniformly to all of the monoids
\mathcal{P}_{n}
,
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{n}
,
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}
and
\mathcal{M}_{n}
. They can be readily adapted to treat
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
, but must be significantly modified for
\mathcal{B}_{n}
.
Although our results completely settle the question for all of the best-studied diagram monoids, we are hopeful that the new techniques we develop will prove useful in further studies of transformation degrees.

The paper is organised as follows. We begin in Section
2
with preliminaries on semigroups and diagram monoids. Section
3
contains general results on transformation representations, and their connections to actions and right congruences.
Sections
4
and
5
then apply this to the monoids
\mathcal{P}_{n}
,
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{n}
,
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}
,
\mathcal{M}_{n}
and
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
. The main results are Theorems
4.1
and
5.1
, which show that the degree of each such monoid is
1+|Q|
, for a suitable set
Q
of low-rank projections. The Brauer monoid
\mathcal{B}_{n}
is treated in Section
6
, where we must use rather different methods; see Theorem
6.1
.
En route to proving these theorems, we construct explicit faithful actions/representations of the stated degree; see Theorems
4.9
,
5.4
,
6.16
and
6.21
. Since every such action contains a global fixed point,
the minimum
partial
transformation degree is given by
\deg^{\prime}(M)=\deg(M)-1
.
Finally, in Section
7
we give combinatorial formulae for
|Q|
for each the monoids
\mathcal{P}_{n}
,
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{n}
,
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}
,
\mathcal{M}_{n}
and
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
.
As mentioned above, formulae and computed values are given in Tables
1
and
2
.

2
Preliminaries

We begin by recalling the preliminary ideas and results we need concerning semigroups (Section
2.1
), regular
*
-semigroups (Section
2.2
) and diagram monoids (Section
2.3
). For more basic background on semigroup theory, see for example
[
32
,
11
]
.

2.1
Semigroups

Let
S
be a semigroup, and let
S^{1}
be the monoid completion of
S
. So
S^{1}=S
if
S
is a monoid, or else
S=S\cup\{1\}
, where
1
is a symbol not belonging to
S
, acting as an adjoined identity element. Green’s
\mathscr{L}
,
\mathscr{R}
and
\mathscr{J}
pre-orders and equivalences are defined, for
a,b\in S
, by

\displaystyle a\leq_{\mathscr{L}}b

\displaystyle\ \Leftrightarrow\ a\in S^{1}b,

\displaystyle a\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}b

\displaystyle\ \Leftrightarrow\ S^{1}a=S^{1}b,

\displaystyle a\leq_{\mathscr{R}}b

\displaystyle\ \Leftrightarrow\ a\in bS^{1},

\displaystyle a\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}b

\displaystyle\ \Leftrightarrow\ aS^{1}=bS^{1},

\displaystyle a\leq_{\mathscr{J}}b

\displaystyle\ \Leftrightarrow\ a\in S^{1}bS^{1},

\displaystyle a\mathrel{\mathscr{J}}b

\displaystyle\ \Leftrightarrow\ S^{1}aS^{1}=S^{1}bS^{1}.

So, for example,
a\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}b
holds when either
a=b
, or else
a=sb
and
b=ta
for some
s,t\in S
; similar comments apply to
\mathscr{R}
and
\mathscr{J}
.
We also have the
\mathscr{H}
and
\mathscr{D}
relations, defined by
\mathscr{H}=\mathscr{L}\cap\mathscr{R}
and
\mathscr{D}=\mathscr{L}\vee\mathscr{R}
, where the latter is the join of
\mathscr{L}
and
\mathscr{R}
in the lattice of all equivalences of
S
. If
S
is finite, then
\mathscr{D}=\mathscr{J}
. The
\mathscr{R}
-class of an element
a\in S
is denoted by
R_{a}
, and similarly for
\mathscr{L}
-classes, etc. The set
S/\mathscr{R}
of all
\mathscr{R}
-classes is partially ordered by

R_{a}\leq R_{b}\ \Leftrightarrow\ a\leq_{\mathscr{R}}b\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $a,b\in S$.}

(2.1)

A semigroup is
stable
if

sa\mathrel{\mathscr{J}}a\ \Leftrightarrow\ sa\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}a\and as\mathrel{\mathscr{J}}a\ \Leftrightarrow\ as\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}a\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $a,s\in S$.}

(2.2)

Any finite semigroup is stable.

An equivalence relation
\sigma
on a semigroup
S
is a
right congruence
if it is
right-compatible
, meaning that

(a,b)\in\sigma\ \Rightarrow\ (as,bs)\in\sigma\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $a,b,s\in S$.}

Left congruences
are defined symmetrically. A
(two-sided) congruence
is an equivalence that is both a left and right congruence. For example,
\mathscr{L}
is a right congruence, and
\mathscr{R}
is a left congruence. The
trivial
and
universal
congruences are respectively denoted

\Delta_{S}=\{{(a,a)}:{a\in S}\}\and\nabla_{S}=S\times S.

For a set of pairs
\Sigma\subseteq S\times S
, we write
\Sigma^{\sharp}
for the (two-sided) congruence of
S
generated by
\Sigma
, i.e. the intersection of all congruences containing
\Sigma
. When
\Sigma=\{(a,b)\}
for some
a,b\in S
, we write
(a,b)^{\sharp}=\Sigma^{\sharp}
; such a congruence is called
principal
.

A
right ideal
of a semigroup
S
is a subset
I\subseteq S
such that
IS\subseteq I
.
Left ideals
and
(two-sided) ideals
are defined analogously.
Any left, right or two-sided ideal is a union of
\mathscr{L}
-,
\mathscr{R}
- or
\mathscr{J}
-classes, respectively.
The
Rees right congruence
associated to a right ideal
I
is defined by

\mathcal{R}_{I}=\nabla_{I}\cup\Delta_{S}=\{{(x,y)\in S\times S}:{x=y\text{ or }x,y\in I}\}.

As special cases we have
\mathcal{R}_{\varnothing}=\Delta_{S}
and
\mathcal{R}_{S}=\nabla_{S}
.

2.2
Regular
*
-semigroups

A
regular
*
-semigroup
is a semigroup
S
with an additional unary operation
S\to S:a\mapsto a^{*}
satisfying the identities

a^{**}=a=aa^{*}a\and(ab)^{*}=b^{*}a^{*}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $a,b\in S$.}

These were introduced by Nordahl and Scheiblich in
[
52
]
, and the diagram monoids considered here are key examples. The set of
projections
of a regular
*
-semigroup
S
is denoted

P(S)=\{{p\in S}:{p^{2}=p=p^{*}}\}.

It is well known (see for example
[
54
]
) that
P(S)=\{{aa^{*}}:{a\in S}\}=\{{a^{*}a}:{a\in S}\}
, and that

a\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}b\ \Leftrightarrow\ a^{*}a=b^{*}b\and a\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}b\ \Leftrightarrow\ aa^{*}=bb^{*}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $a,b\in S$.}

(2.3)

Since every element of
S
is
\mathscr{R}
-related to a unique projection (namely
a\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}aa^{*}
), with a similar statement for the
\mathscr{L}
relation, it follows that any
\mathscr{D}
-class
D
of
S
contains
|D/\mathscr{L}|=|D/\mathscr{R}|
projections, and that
|P(S)|=|S/\mathscr{L}|=|S/\mathscr{R}|
.

2.3
Diagram monoids

Fix a non-negative integer
n\geq 0
, and write
{\bf n}=\{1,\ldots,n\}
and
{\bf n}^{\prime}=\{1^{\prime},\ldots,n^{\prime}\}
. The
partition monoid

\mathcal{P}_{n}
is the set of all set partitions of
{\bf n}\cup{\bf n}^{\prime}
. Such a partition is identified with any graph on vertex set
{\bf n}\cup{\bf n}^{\prime}
whose connected components are the blocks of the partition; the vertices from
{\bf n}
and
{\bf n}^{\prime}
are drawn on an upper and lower row, respectively, ordered
1<\cdots<n
and
1^{\prime}<\cdots<n^{\prime}
.

The product of
\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
is defined as follows. We first write
{\bf n}^{\prime\prime}=\{1^{\prime\prime},\ldots,n^{\prime\prime}\}
, and we let:

•

\alpha^{\vee}
be the graph on vertex set
{\bf n}\cup{\bf n}^{\prime\prime}
obtained by changing each lower vertex
x^{\prime}
of
\alpha
to
x^{\prime\prime}
,

•

\beta^{\wedge}
be the graph on vertex set
{\bf n}^{\prime\prime}\cup{\bf n}^{\prime}
obtained by changing each upper vertex
x
of
\beta
to
x^{\prime\prime}
,

•

\Pi(\alpha,\beta)
be the graph on vertex set
{\bf n}\cup{\bf n}^{\prime\prime}\cup{\bf n}^{\prime}
whose edge set is the union of the edge sets of
\alpha^{\vee}
and
\beta^{\wedge}
.

We call
\Pi(\alpha,\beta)
the
product graph
of
\alpha
and
\beta
, and we draw it with vertices
1^{\prime\prime}<\cdots<n^{\prime\prime}
in a new middle row. The product
\alpha\beta\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
is then the unique partition of
{\bf n}\cup{\bf n}^{\prime}
such that
x,y\in{\bf n}\cup{\bf n}^{\prime}
belong to the same block of
\alpha\beta
if and only if they belong to the same connected component of
\Pi(\alpha,\beta)
. An example calculation is given in Figure
1
in the case
n=6
. The identity element of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
is
\operatorname{id}_{n}={\lower 6.02773pt\hbox{
\hbox to29.02pt{\vbox to20.49pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-6.82875pt\lower-1.70715pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

}}
, and the group of units is (isomorphic to) the symmetric group
\mathcal{S}_{n}
.

=\alpha\beta

Multiplication of partitions
\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal{P}_{6}
, with the product graph
\Pi(\alpha,\beta)
in the middle.

We now recall the definitions of the submonoids of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
we will work with. The first two are:

•

\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{n}=\{{\alpha\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}:{\text{each block of $\alpha$ has size $\leq 2$}}\}
, the
partial Brauer monoid
,

•

\mathcal{B}_{n}=\{{\alpha\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}:{\text{each block of $\alpha$ has size $2$}}\}
, the
Brauer monoid
.

A partition from
\mathcal{P}_{n}
is
planar
if it is represented by a graph whose edges are all contained within the rectangle spanned by the vertices, and have no crossings. For example,
\beta\in\mathcal{P}_{6}
from Figure
1
is planar, but
\alpha
is not. We then have the further three submonoids of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
:

•

\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}=\{{\alpha\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}:{\alpha\text{ is planar}}\}
, the
planar partition monoid
,

•

\mathcal{M}_{n}=\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}\cap\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{n}
, the
Motzkin monoid
,

•

\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}=\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}\cap\mathcal{B}_{n}
, the
Temperley–Lieb monoid
(sometimes called the
Jones monoid
).

The containments among these monoids, along with sample elements, are shown in Figure
2
. It is well known (see for example
[
35
,
28
]
) that
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}\cong\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{2n}
, via an isomorphism
\alpha\mapsto\widetilde{\alpha}
illustrated in Figure
3
.

\mathcal{P}_{n}
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{n}
\mathcal{B}_{n}
\mathcal{S}_{n}
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}
\mathcal{M}_{n}
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
\operatorname{id}_{n}

Figure 2:
Submonoids of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
(left) and representative elements from each submonoid (right).

Figure 3:
A planar partition
\alpha\in\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{8}
(black), with its corresponding Temperley–Lieb element
{\widetilde{\alpha}\in\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{16}}
(orange), illustrating the isomorphism
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}\to\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{2n}
.

A non-empty subset
\varnothing\not=X\subseteq{\bf n}\cup{\bf n}^{\prime}
is called:

•

an
upper non-transversal
if
X\subseteq{\bf n}
, i.e. if
X
contains only un-dashed vertices,

•

a
lower non-transversal
if
X\subseteq{\bf n}^{\prime}
, i.e. if
X
contains only dashed vertices, or

•

a
transversal
otherwise, i.e. if
X
contains both dashed and un-dashed vertices.

For
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
we define

•

\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)=\{{x\in{\bf n}}:{x\text{ is contained in a transversal of }\alpha}\}
, the
domain
of
\alpha
,

•

\operatorname{codom}(\alpha)=\{{x\in{\bf n}}:{x^{\prime}\text{ is contained in a transversal of }\alpha}\}
, the
codomain
of
\alpha
,

•

\ker(\alpha)=\{{(x,y)\in{\bf n}\times{\bf n}}:{x\text{ and }y\text{ are contained in the same block of }\alpha}\}
, the
kernel
of
\alpha
,

•

\operatorname{coker}(\alpha)=\{{(x,y)\in{\bf n}\times{\bf n}}:{x^{\prime}\text{ and }y^{\prime}\text{ are contained in the same block of }\alpha}\}
, the
cokernel
of
\alpha
.

We also define
\operatorname{rank}(\alpha)
, the
rank
of
\alpha
, to be the number of transversals of
\alpha
. For example, with
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}_{6}
as in Figure
1
, we have (using an obvious notation for equivalences):

\displaystyle\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\{2,3\},

\displaystyle\ker(\alpha)

\displaystyle=(1,4\mid 2,3\mid 5,6),

\displaystyle\operatorname{codom}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\{4,5\},

\displaystyle\operatorname{coker}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=(1,2,6\mid 3\mid 4,5),

\displaystyle\operatorname{rank}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=1.

For
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
we use the tabular notation

\alpha=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{1}&\cdots&B_{r}&D_{1}&\cdots&D_{t}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)

(2.4)

to indicate that
\alpha
has transversals
A_{i}\cup B_{i}^{\prime}
(
i=1,\ldots,r
), upper non-transversals
C_{i}
(
i=1,\ldots,s
) and lower non-transversals
D_{i}^{\prime}
(
i=1,\ldots,t
). Note that one or two of
r,s,t
could be
0
in (
2.4
), but not all three (unless
n=0
).
When we use this tabular notation, we always assume that the transversals are ordered so that
{\min(A_{1})<\cdots<\min(A_{r})}
.
When
\alpha\in\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}
is planar, this in fact implies
A_{1}<\cdots<A_{r}
and
B_{1}<\cdots<B_{r}
. (Here for subsets
X,Y\subseteq{\bf n}
we write
X<Y
to indicate that
x<y
for all
x\in X
and
y\in Y
.)

For
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
as in (
2.4
), we define

\alpha^{*}=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{1}&\cdots&B_{r}&D_{1}&\cdots&D_{t}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr),

which is the partition obtained by interchanging dashed and un-dashed elements of
{\bf n}\cup{\bf n}^{\prime}
. Diagrammatically,
\alpha^{*}
is obtained by reflecting any graph representing
\alpha
in a horizontal axis. For example, with
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}_{6}
from Figure
1
we have
\alpha^{*}={\lower 6.02773pt\hbox{

}}
. It is easy to see that this gives
\mathcal{P}_{n}
the structure of a regular
*
-monoid, i.e. that

\alpha^{**}=\alpha=\alpha\alpha^{*}\alpha\and(\alpha\beta)^{*}=\beta^{*}\alpha^{*}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal{P}_{n}$.}

The projections of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
, i.e. the elements
\varepsilon\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
satisfying
\varepsilon^{2}=\varepsilon=\varepsilon^{*}
, have the form

\varepsilon=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr).

Each of
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{n}
,
\mathcal{B}_{n}
,
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}
,
\mathcal{M}_{n}
and
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
is a regular
*
-submonoid of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
. So too is
\mathcal{S}_{n}
, in which we have
\alpha^{*}=\alpha^{-1}
.

3
Transformation monoids and representations

For a set
X
, the
full transformation monoid

\mathcal{T}_{X}
consists of all self-maps of
X
under composition. For
f\in\mathcal{T}_{X}
and
x\in X
we write
xf
for the image of
x
under
f
, and compose transformations left to right. When
X=\{1,\ldots,n\}
for a positive integer
n
, we write
\mathcal{T}_{X}=\mathcal{T}_{n}
.

A
transformation representation
of a semigroup
S
is a homomorphism
S\to\mathcal{T}_{X}
for some set
X
. When the representation is injective we say it is
faithful
, and that
S

embeds
in
\mathcal{T}_{X}
. Cayley’s Theorem states that any semigroup
S
embeds in
\mathcal{T}_{S^{1}}
; the proof is the observation that
S
acts faithfully on
S^{1}
by right translations
[
32
, Theorem 1.1.2]
. The
minimum transformation degree
of a finite semigroup
S
is defined to be

\deg(S)=\min\{{n\geq 1}:{S\text{ embeds in }\mathcal{T}_{n}}\}.

Our main goal in this paper is to compute this degree parameter for several families of diagram monoids. In this section we establish some of the theoretical groundwork for doing this.
We begin by recalling the connections between transformation representations and actions (Section
3.1
), partial representations (Section
3.2
) and right congruences (Section
3.3
).
In Section
3.4
we give a useful construction of right congruences from
\mathscr{R}
-classes.
Finally, we show in Section
3.5
how to build families of (partial) actions on projections of regular
*
-semigroups.

3.1
Transformation representations and actions

In all that follows, it will be convenient to view transformation representations and degrees through the lens of actions. For more detailed background, we refer to
[
36
]
.

Recall that a
(right) action
of a semigroup
S
on a set
X
is a map

\mu:X\times S\to X\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for which}\hskip 20.00003pt\mu(\mu(x,a),b)=\mu(x,ab)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $x\in X$ and $a,b\in S$.}

We write
\mu
to the left of its arguments for readability. It is standard to abbreviate
\mu(x,a)
to
xa
, in which case the defining property above says
(xa)b=x(ab)
. However, we will only occasionally use such shorthand notation, and will always indicate when we are doing so. One reason for this is that we will often have to deal with several actions at once. Another reason is that a semigroup
S
will often act on a subset
X\subseteq S
in such a way that
\mu(x,a)
is
not
the product
xa
of
x
and
a
in
S
.
In the literature,
X
is often referred to as an
S
-act
or an
S
-set
.
The
degree
of the action
\mu
is defined to be
|X|
.
We say
\mu
is a
monoid action
if
S
is a monoid with identity
1
and
\mu(x,1)=x
for all
x
.

Given an action
\mu:X\times S\to X
, a subset
Y\subseteq X
is called a
sub-act
if
\mu(y,a)\in Y
for all
y\in Y
and
a\in S
. In this case,
\mu
restricts to an action
\mu{\restriction}_{Y}:Y\times S\to Y
.
For an arbitrary subset
Y
of
X
, we define the sub-act

\langle Y\rangle_{\mu}=Y\cup\{{\mu(y,a)}:{y\in Y,\ a\in S}\},

which we call the
sub-act generated by
Y
. (Of course ‘
Y\cup{}
’ can be deleted if
\mu
is a monoid action.) If
X=\langle x\rangle_{\mu}
for some
x\in X
, we say that
\mu
is
monogenic
. Monogenic acts are also called
cyclic
or
strictly cyclic
in the literature

Consider actions
\mu_{1}:X_{1}\times S\to X_{1}
and
\mu_{2}:X_{2}\times S\to X_{2}
. We say these are
isomorphic
, and write
\mu_{1}\cong\mu_{2}
, if there is a bijection
\xi:X_{1}\to X_{2}
such that

(\mu_{1}(x,a))\xi=\mu_{2}(x\xi,a)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $x\in X_{1}$ and $a\in S$.}

We say sub-acts
Y_{1}\subseteq X_{1}
and
Y_{2}\subseteq X_{2}
are isomorphic, and write
Y_{1}\cong Y_{2}
if the restrictions are isomorphic:
\mu_{1}{\restriction}_{Y_{1}}\cong\mu_{2}{\restriction}_{Y_{2}}
.

Consider again actions
\mu_{1}:X_{1}\times S\to X_{1}
and
\mu_{2}:X_{2}\times S\to X_{2}
, where this time we assume that
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
are disjoint. The (disjoint) union

\mu_{1}\sqcup\mu_{2}:(X_{1}\sqcup X_{2})\times S\to X_{1}\sqcup X_{2}

is then an action. More generally, suppose we have isomorphic sub-acts
Y_{1}\subseteq X_{1}
and
Y_{2}\subseteq X_{2}
, as witnessed by the bijection
\xi:Y_{1}\to Y_{2}
. We define
X=X_{1}\sqcup_{\xi}X_{2}
to be the quotient of
X_{1}\sqcup X_{2}
by the equivalence relation that equates
y
and
y\xi
for each
y\in Y_{1}
. Writing
[x]\in X
for the equivalence class of each
x\in X_{1}\sqcup X_{2}
, we then have a well-defined action

\mu:X\times S\to X\hskip 20.00003pt\text{given by}\hskip 20.00003pt\mu([x],a)=\begin{cases}[\mu_{1}(x,a)]&\text{if $x\in X_{1}$}\\
[\mu_{2}(x,a)]&\text{if $x\in X_{2}$.}\end{cases}

(3.1)

This action
\mu
is called the
push-out along
\xi
of
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
, and is denoted by
\mu_{1}\sqcup_{\xi}\mu_{2}
.

Given an action
\mu:X\times S\to X
, one can define a transformation representation

\displaystyle\phi_{\mu}:S\to\mathcal{T}_{X}:a\mapsto f_{a},

where

\displaystyle xf_{a}

\displaystyle=\mu(x,a)

for
a\in S
and
x\in X
.

Conversely, given a transformation representation
\phi:S\to\mathcal{T}_{X}:a\mapsto f_{a}
, one can define an action

\displaystyle\mu_{\phi}:X\times S\to X,

where

\displaystyle\mu_{\phi}(x,a)

\displaystyle=xf_{a}

for
a\in S
and
x\in X
.

These constructions are mutually inverse.

The
kernel
of an action
\mu:X\times S\to X
, denoted
\ker(\mu)
, is defined to be the kernel of the corresponding representation
\phi_{\mu}:S\to\mathcal{T}_{X}
, so

\ker(\mu)=\ker(\phi_{\mu})=\{{(a,b)\in S\times S}:{\mu(x,a)=\mu(x,b)\text{ for all }x\in X}\}.

We say the action
\mu
is
faithful
if
\phi_{\mu}
is faithful, which is equivalent to
\ker(\mu)=\Delta_{S}
. Thus, we also have

\deg(S)=\min\{{n\geq 1}:{S\text{ has a faithful action of degree }n}\}.

3.2
Partial actions and representations

The
partial transformation monoid

\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
consists of all partial self-maps of
X
under (relational) composition. Let
-
be a symbol not belonging to
X
, and write
X^{-}=X\cup\{-\}
. Then
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
is isomorphic to the submonoid of
\mathcal{T}_{X^{-}}
consisting of all transformations that fix
-
. In particular, we have embeddings
\mathcal{T}_{n}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{n}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{T}_{n+1}
for any
n
.

One can of course speak of partial transformation representations and degrees. Denote the
minimum partial transformation degree
of a finite semigroup
S
by

\deg^{\prime}(S)=\min\{{n\geq 1}:{S\text{ embeds in }\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{n}}\}.

It follows from the above-mentioned embeddings that
\deg^{\prime}(S)\leq\deg(S)\leq\deg^{\prime}(S)+1
, and that
\deg^{\prime}(S)=\deg(S)-1
holds precisely when there is a minimum-degree faithful transformation representation with a global fixed point. This will be the case for every representation/action we construct in Sections
4
–
6
.

3.3
Actions and right congruences

One special family of actions (and hence transformation representations) come from right congruences, and these will be especially important in the current work. To describe them, fix some right congruence
\sigma
on a semigroup
S
. Write
[x]_{\sigma}
for the
\sigma
-class of
x\in S
, and let
S/\sigma=\{{[x]_{\sigma}}:{x\in S}\}
be the set of all such classes. We then have a well-defined action

\mu_{\sigma}:(S/\sigma)\times S\to S/\sigma\hskip 20.00003pt\text{given by}\hskip 20.00003pt\mu_{\sigma}([x]_{\sigma},a)=[xa]_{\sigma}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $a,x\in S$.}

When
S
is a monoid,
\mu_{\sigma}
is generated by
[1]_{\sigma}
, so is monogenic. Conversely, we have the following, which is a special case of
[
67
, Proposition 1.1]
. We say an action
\mu
is a
right congruence action
if
\mu\cong\mu_{\sigma}
for some right congruence
\sigma
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Any monogenic monoid action is a right congruence action.

The
minimum right congruence degree
of
S
is defined by

\deg_{\operatorname{rc}}(S)=\min\{{n\geq 1}:{S\text{ has a faithful right congruence action of degree }n}\}.

Of course we have
\deg(S)\leq\deg_{\operatorname{rc}}(S)
.

The next result is a special case of
[
67
, Proposition 1.2]
, formulated in a way that is convenient for our purposes.

Proposition 3.3
.

If
\sigma
is a right congruence of a monoid
S
, then
\ker(\mu_{\sigma})
is the largest two-sided congruence of
S
contained in
\sigma
. Consequently,
\mu_{\sigma}
is faithful if and only if
\sigma
contains no non-trivial two-sided congruences.

We say that an action
\mu:X\times S\to X

separates
a pair
(a,b)\in S\times S
if
\mu(x,a)\not=\mu(x,b)
for some
x\in X
. This is equivalent to the transformations
f_{a},f_{b}\in\mathcal{T}_{X}
under the associated transformation representation
\phi_{\mu}:S\to\mathcal{T}_{X}
being different.

A two-sided congruence
\sigma
of
S
is
minimal
if
\sigma\not=\Delta_{S}
, and the only congruence properly contained in
\sigma
is
\Delta_{S}
. Such a congruence is necessarily principal. If
S
is finite, then every non-trivial congruence contains a minimal congruence.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
S
be a finite monoid, and let
\Gamma\subseteq S\times S
be such that
\{{(a,b)^{\sharp}}:{(a,b)\in\Gamma}\}
consists of all the minimal two-sided congruences of
S
. Then a semigroup action
\mu:X\times S\to X
is faithful if and only if it separates each pair from
\Gamma
.

Proof.

If a pair
(a,b)\in\Gamma
was not separated by
\mu
, then we would have
\mu(x,a)=\mu(x,b)
for all
x\in X
, meaning that
(a,b)\in\ker(\mu)
. But then
\ker(\mu)\not=\Delta_{S}
, so
\mu
is not faithful.

Conversely, suppose
\mu
is not faithful, so that
\ker(\mu)\not=\Delta_{S}
. We can then fix some minimal congruence
\sigma\subseteq\ker(\mu)
, and by assumption we have
\sigma=(a,b)^{\sharp}
for some
(a,b)\in\Gamma
. Since
(a,b)\in\sigma\subseteq\ker(\mu)
we have
\mu(x,a)=\mu(x,b)
for all
x\in X
, so that
\mu
does not separate
(a,b)
.
∎

We say an equivalence relation
\sigma
on a set
A

separates
a subset
B\subseteq A
if
(a,b)\not\in\sigma
for distinct
{a,b\in B}
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
S
be a semigroup, and suppose
a,b\in S
and
C\subseteq S
are such that any right congruence on
S
separating
\{a,b\}
also separates
C
.
Also let
\mu:X\times S\to X
be an action with
\mu(x,a)\not=\mu(x,b)
for some (fixed)
x\in X
. Then the map
C\to X:c\mapsto\mu(x,c)
is injective.

Proof.

It is easy to check that the relation
\sigma=\{{(s,t)\in S\times S}:{\mu(x,s)=\mu(x,t)}\}
is a right congruence. The assumption
\mu(x,a)\not=\mu(x,b)
says that
\sigma
separates
\{a,b\}
, so it follows that
\sigma
separates
C
. But this says precisely that the stated map is injective.
∎

3.4
Right congruences from
\mathscr{R}
-classes

For some of our later applications, we show how to build congruences on a semigroup starting from a specified
\mathscr{R}
-class. These will have the form
\mathcal{R}_{I}\vee\mathscr{L}^{U}
for a carefully chosen right ideal
I
and subsemigroup
U
of
S
. Here
\mathcal{R}_{I}
is a Rees right congruence, and
\mathscr{L}^{U}
denotes
Green’s relative
\mathscr{L}
relation
, introduced in
[
68
]
, and defined for
a,b\in S
by

a\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}^{U}b\ \Leftrightarrow\ U^{1}a=U^{1}b.

This is again a right congruence, and as special cases we have
\mathscr{L}^{S}=\mathscr{L}
and
\mathscr{L}^{\varnothing}=\Delta_{S}
.

Throughout this section, let
R
be a fixed
\mathscr{R}
-class of a semigroup
S
. Define the sets

T=\{{a\in S}:{aR\subseteq R}\},\hskip 20.00003ptK=\{{a\in S}:{R\leq R_{a}}\}\and I=S\setminus K=\{{a\in S}:{R\not\leq R_{a}}\},

(3.6)

where
\leq
is the ordering on
\mathscr{R}
-classes in (
2.1
). It is easy to check that
T
is a (possibly empty) subsemigroup of
S
, and that
I
is a (possibly empty) right ideal.

Lemma 3.7
.

If
z
is an arbitrary element of
R
, then

(i)

K=\{{a\in S}:{z\leq_{\mathscr{R}}a}\}
,

(ii)

T=\{{a\in S}:{az\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}z}\}
,

(iii)

T=\{{a\in S}:{az=z}\}
if
S
is stable and
R
is
\mathscr{H}
-trivial.

Proof.

(i)
. Since
R=R_{z}
, this follows immediately from the definitions.

(ii)
. Let
a\in S
. If
aR\subseteq R
, then
az\in aR\subseteq R=R_{z}
, which says
az\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}z
.

Conversely, suppose
az\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}z
, and let
x\in R
be arbitrary, so that
x\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}z
. Since
\mathscr{R}
is a left congruence it follows that
ax\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}az\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}z
, which says
ax\in R
, showing that
aR\subseteq R
.

(iii)
. Suppose
S
is stable and
R
is
\mathscr{H}
-trivial. Given part
(ii)
, it is enough to show that
{az\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}z\ \Rightarrow\ az=z}
. So suppose
az\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}z
. Since
\mathscr{R}\subseteq\mathscr{J}
we have
az\mathrel{\mathscr{J}}z
, and it follows from stability (see (
2.2
)) that
az\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}z
, and then from
\mathscr{H}
-triviality that
az=z
.
∎

In what follows, we typically use parts
(i)
and
(ii)
of Lemma
3.7
without explicit reference. If
\sigma
is an equivalence on a set
X
, then for any subset
Y\subseteq X
we denote by
\sigma{\restriction}_{Y}=\sigma\cap(Y\times Y)
the restriction of
\sigma
to
Y
.

Lemma 3.8
.

For any subsemigroup
U\subseteq T
, we have
\mathscr{L}^{U}\subseteq\nabla_{I}\cup\nabla_{K}
, and consequently
{\mathscr{L}^{U}=\mathscr{L}^{U}{\restriction}_{I}\cup\mathscr{L}^{U}{\restriction}_{K}}
.

Proof.

Let
(a,b)\in\mathscr{L}^{U}
. By symmetry, and since
\mathscr{L}^{U}
is an equivalence, it suffices to show that
a\in K\ \Rightarrow\ b\in K
. So suppose
a\in K
. Also let
z\in R
, so that
z\leq_{\mathscr{R}}a
, which gives
z=as
for some
s\in S^{1}
. Since
(a,b)\in\mathscr{L}^{U}
we have
b=ua
for some
u\in U^{1}
. Since
U\subseteq T
we have
uz\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}z
. It follows that
z\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}uz=uas=bs\leq_{\mathscr{R}}b
, which gives
z\leq_{\mathscr{R}}b
, i.e.
b\in K
.
∎

With
R
,
T
,
K
and
I
as above, and for any subsemigroup
U\subseteq T
, we define the right congruence

\sigma=\mathcal{R}_{I}\vee\mathscr{L}^{U},

where here
\mathcal{R}_{I}=\nabla_{I}\cup\Delta_{S}=\nabla_{I}\cup\Delta_{K}
is the Rees right congruence, and where
\vee
denotes the join in the lattice of equivalences of
S
. It follows from Lemma
3.8
that in fact

\sigma=\nabla_{I}\cup\mathscr{L}^{U}{\restriction}_{K}=\big\{{(a,b)}:{a,b\in I\text{ or }[a,b\in K\text{ and }U^{1}a=U^{1}b]}\big\}.

(3.9)

In particular, we have
S/\sigma=\{I\}\cup(K/\mathscr{L}^{U})
.

3.5
Regular
*
-semigroups and (partial) actions on projections

We now show how the projections of a regular
*
-semigroup can be used to define (partial) actions, and hence transformation representations.

Let
S
be a regular
*
-semigroup, and let
P=P(S)=\{{p\in S}:{p^{2}=p=p^{*}}\}
be its set of projections. For
p\in P
and
a\in S
we write

p^{a}=a^{*}pa=(pa)^{*}pa\in P.

Since
(p^{a})^{b}=p^{ab}
for all
p\in P
and
a,b\in S
, this defines an action

P\times S\to P:(p,a)\mapsto p^{a}.

(3.10)

Now suppose
Q\subseteq P
is a set of projections that is closed under the action (
3.10
), meaning that
p^{a}\in Q
for all
p\in Q
and
a\in S
. Also suppose
\preceq
is a left-compatible pre-order on
S
containing
\leq_{\mathscr{R}}
, meaning that:

•

\preceq
is reflexive and transitive,

•

a\preceq b\ \Rightarrow\ sa\preceq sb
for all
a,b,s\in S
, and

•

as\preceq a
for all
a,s\in S
.

Let
{\approx}={\preceq}\cap{\succeq}
be the equivalence on
S
induced by
\preceq
, so that
a\approx b
if and only if
a\preceq b
and
{b\preceq a}
. Note that
\approx
is a left congruence containing
\mathscr{R}
. Let
-
be a symbol not belonging to
P
, let
Q^{-}=Q\cup\{-\}
, and define

\mu:Q^{-}\times S\to Q^{-}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{by}\hskip 20.00003pt\mu(p,a)=\begin{cases}p^{a}&\text{if $p\in Q$ and $p\approx pa$}\\
-&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(3.11)

So in particular
\mu(-,a)=-
for all
a\in S
.

Proposition 3.12
.

If
S
is a regular
*
-semigroup, and if
Q
,
\preceq
and
\approx
are as above, then (
3.11
) determines an action
\mu:Q^{-}\times S\to Q^{-}
. If
S
is a monoid, then
\mu
is a monoid action.

Proof.

For the first assertion (the second is clear), we must show that

\mu(p,ab)=\mu(\mu(p,a),b)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $p\in Q^{-}$ and $a,b\in S$.}

This is clear if
p=-
, so for the rest of the proof we assume that
p\in Q
. Given that (
3.10
) determines an action of
S
on
Q
, it is in fact enough to show that

\mu(p,ab)\in Q\hskip 20.00003pt\Leftrightarrow\hskip 20.00003pt\mu(\mu(p,a),b)\in Q.

Following the definitions, this amounts to showing that

p\approx pab\hskip 20.00003pt\Leftrightarrow\hskip 20.00003pt[p\approx pa\text{ and }p^{a}\approx p^{a}b].

For the forward implication, suppose
p\approx pab
. Since
{\leq_{\mathscr{R}}}\subseteq{\preceq}
, we then have

p\approx pab\preceq pa\preceq p,

so that
p\approx pa\approx pab
. From
pa\approx pab
we have
a^{*}pa\approx a^{*}pab
(as
\approx
is left-compatible), i.e.
p^{a}\approx p^{a}b
.

Conversely, suppose
p\approx pa
and
p^{a}\approx p^{a}b
. From the latter (and left-compatibility) we have
pa\cdot p^{a}\approx pa\cdot p^{a}b
. But

pa\cdot p^{a}=pa\cdot a^{*}pa=pa\cdot(pa)^{*}\cdot pa=pa,

so the previous conclusion says
pa\approx pab
. Combined with
p\approx pa
, it follows that
p\approx pab
.
∎

Remark 3.13
.

(i)

One obvious choice for the pre-order
\preceq
is
\leq_{\mathscr{R}}
itself, in which case
\approx
is
\mathscr{R}
. Also taking the obvious
Q=P
, this leads to the action

P^{-}\times S\to P^{-}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{given by}\hskip 20.00003pt(p,a)\mapsto\begin{cases}p^{a}&\text{if $p\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}pa$}\\
-&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(3.14)

It turns out that this is equivalent to a well-known construction in the
semilocal theory
of finite semigroups; see
[
4
, Chapters 7 and 8]
,
[
56
, Section 4.6]
and
[
44
, Appendix A]
. First consider the action of a
finite
regular
*
-semigroup
S
on
P=P(S)
given in (
3.10
). We have already observed that the set
{S/{\mathscr{L}}}
of
\mathscr{L}
-classes of
S
is in one-one correspondence with
P
, via
p\mapsto L_{p}
. Since
pa\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}a^{*}pa=p^{a}
for all
p\in P
and
a\in S
(as
(pa)^{*}pa=p^{a}
; cf. (
2.3
)), it follows that this action is equivalent to that of
S
on
{S/{\mathscr{L}}}
given by
(L_{p},a)\mapsto L_{pa}
. For any
\mathscr{D}
-class
D
of
S
, this restricts to a partial action of
S
on
{D/{\mathscr{L}}}
given by

(L_{p},a)\mapsto\begin{cases}L_{pa}=L_{p^{a}}&\text{if $pa\in D$}\\
-&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

This is the
Right Letter Mapping (RLM)
action associated to
D
.
Since
S
is stable (as it is finite), the condition
pa\in D(=D_{p})
is equivalent to
pa\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}p
, and hence this RLM action of
S
on
{D/{\mathscr{L}}}
is equivalent to that of
S
on
P(D)^{-}
given by

(p,a)\mapsto\begin{cases}p^{a}&\text{if $p\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}pa$}\\
-&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

The union of these actions over all
\mathscr{D}
-classes
D
of
S
(amalgamating the common fixed point,
-
, of each) is precisely that given above in (
3.14
).

Finally, it is also worth noting that in the special case of an
inverse
semigroup
S
, the partial transformation representation
S\to\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{P}
associated to the action in (
3.14
) is precisely Munn’s representation of
S
, used in his construction of
fundamental
inverse semigroups
[
51
]
.

(ii)

Another obvious choice for
\preceq
is
\nabla_{S}
, in which case
\approx
is also equal to
\nabla_{S}
. Again taking
Q=P
, and in this case keeping in mind that
p\approx pa
for all
p\in P
and
a\in S
, the action (
3.11
) essentially reduces to (
3.10
), with the symbol
-
acting as an adjoined fixed point.

Remark 3.15
.

Keeping Remark
3.13
(i)
in mind, one could think of the results of this section as an extension of certain aspects of the semilocal theory, in the special case of regular
*
-semigroups. It would be interesting to consider this in more generality. As noted in the aforementioned remark, one could replace the conjugation action on projections by the standard action on
\mathscr{L}
-classes,
(L_{x},a)\mapsto L_{xa}
(which exists in any semigroup), and one would need a left-compatible pre-order
\preceq
, as above. In the case of partition monoids, a useful such pre-order comes from a so-called
Ehresmann structure

[
41
,
22
]
, as we note in Section
4.1
, and this could be a natural starting point for more general investigations.

Remark 3.16
.

(Partial) actions on projections will be used in the next two sections to construct minimum-degree faithful representations/actions for many of our diagram monoids. However, it is worth noting that the degree of a general regular
*
-semigroup need not have anything to do with the number of its projections. For example, a billion-by-billion rectangular band has a billion projections, but degree
64

[
9
]
.

4
The partition, partial Brauer, planar partition and Motzkin monoids

With the theoretical results of Section
3
now established, we now come to our main task: the calculation of the minimum transformation degrees of our diagram monoids.

Throughout this section, we let
M
denote any of the partition monoid
\mathcal{P}_{n}
, the partial Brauer monoid
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{n}
, the planar partition monoid
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}
or the Motzkin monoid
\mathcal{M}_{n}
. The Temperley–Lieb monoid
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
will be treated in Section
5
, and the Brauer monoid
\mathcal{B}_{n}
in Section
6
. Our goal here is to prove the following:

Theorem 4.1
.

If
n\geq 2
, and if
M
is any of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
,
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{n}
,
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}
or
\mathcal{M}_{n}
, then

\deg^{\prime}(M)=|Q|\and\deg(M)=\deg_{\operatorname{rc}}(M)=1+|Q|,

where
Q=\{{\varepsilon\in P(M)}:{\operatorname{rank}(\varepsilon)\leq 2}\}
. Formulae for
|Q|
can be found in Propositions
7.4
–
7.7
.

To prove the theorem, we show that
1+|Q|
is an upper bound for
\deg_{\operatorname{rc}}(M)
in Section
4.1
(see Theorem
4.9
), and a lower bound for
\deg(M)
in Section
4.2
(see Proposition
4.14
). In fact, Theorem
4.9
gives an explicit right congruence action of degree
1+|Q|
. Since this action has a global fixed point it will follow (as explained in Section
3.2
) that
\deg^{\prime}(M)=\deg(M)-1
.

The proof of the theorem requires some understanding of the (two-sided) congruences of
M
(being
\mathcal{P}_{n}
,
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{n}
,
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}
or
\mathcal{M}_{n}
). These were classified in
[
23
, Theorems 5.4, 6.1 and 7.3]
. We do not need the full classification here, but we do need to know that
M
has precisely three
minimal
(non-trivial) congruences:

\displaystyle\lambda

\displaystyle=\Delta_{M}\cup\{{(\alpha,\beta)\in M\times M}:{\operatorname{rank}(\alpha)=\operatorname{rank}(\beta)=0,\ \alpha\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}\beta}\},

\displaystyle\rho

\displaystyle=\Delta_{M}\cup\{{(\alpha,\beta)\in M\times M}:{\operatorname{rank}(\alpha)=\operatorname{rank}(\beta)=0,\ \alpha\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}\beta}\},

\displaystyle\eta

\displaystyle=\Delta_{M}\cup\{{(\alpha,\beta)\in M\times M}:{\operatorname{rank}(\alpha),\operatorname{rank}(\beta)\leq 1,\ \widehat{\alpha}=\widehat{\beta}}\}.

(4.2)

(These were respectively denoted
\lambda_{0}
,
\rho_{0}
and
\mu_{1}
in
[
23
]
.) The congruence
\eta
involves the mapping

\mathcal{P}_{n}\to\mathcal{P}_{n}:\alpha=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{1}&\cdots&B_{r}&D_{1}&\cdots&D_{t}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\mapsto\widehat{\alpha}=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{1}&\cdots&B_{r}&D_{1}&\cdots&D_{t}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr).

Since the above congruences are minimal, they are generated by any non-trivial pair they contain. Thus, for example, we have

\lambda=(\zeta,\alpha)^{\sharp},\hskip 20.00003pt\rho=(\zeta,\beta)^{\sharp}\and\eta=(\zeta,\gamma)^{\sharp},

(4.3)

in terms of the partitions

(4.4)

Note here that
\beta=\alpha^{*}
(and
\alpha=\beta^{*}
), while
\zeta=\zeta^{*}
and
\gamma=\gamma^{*}
.
Figure
4
shows a Hasse diagram of the congruence lattice
\operatorname{Cong}(M)
, which is the set of all congruences of
M
, ordered by inclusion. For more detailed diagrams see
[
23
, Figures 5 and 6]
.

Figure 4:
The congruence lattice
\operatorname{Cong}(M)
, for
M=\mathcal{P}_{n}
or
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{n}
(left), and for
M=\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}
or
\mathcal{M}_{n}
(right).

4.1
Upper bound

Recall that
M
denotes any of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
,
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{n}
,
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}
or
\mathcal{M}_{n}
. For the rest of this section we write

P=P(M)=\{{\varepsilon\in M}:{\varepsilon^{2}=\varepsilon=\varepsilon^{*}}\}\and P_{r}=\{{\varepsilon\in P}:{\operatorname{rank}(\varepsilon)=r}\}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $0\leq r\leq n$.}

We also define

Q=Q(M)=P_{0}\cup P_{1}\cup P_{2}=\{{\varepsilon\in P}:{\operatorname{rank}(\varepsilon)\leq 2}\}.

(4.5)

Since
\operatorname{rank}(\varepsilon^{\alpha})=\operatorname{rank}(\alpha^{*}\varepsilon\alpha)\leq\operatorname{rank}(\varepsilon)
for all
\varepsilon\in P
and
\alpha\in M
, it is clear that
Q
is closed under the action (
3.10
).

We now define the relation
\preceq
on
M
by

\alpha\preceq\beta\ \Leftrightarrow\ \ker(\alpha)\supseteq\ker(\beta)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $\alpha,\beta\in M$.}

(4.6)

This relation played a crucial role in
[
22
]
, in connection with so-called
Ehresmann structures
on
\mathcal{P}_{n}
. Of particular relevance to the current situation, it was shown in the proof of
[
22
, Lemma 4.6]
that
\preceq
is left-compatible, and it is clearly transitive. We obtain
{\leq_{\mathscr{R}}}\subseteq{\preceq}
from the identity
\ker(\alpha\beta)\supseteq\ker(\alpha)
. It then follows from Proposition
3.12
that we have an action

\mu:Q^{-}\times M\to Q^{-}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{given by}\hskip 20.00003pt\mu(\varepsilon,\alpha)=\begin{cases}\varepsilon^{\alpha}&\text{if $\varepsilon\in Q$ and $\ker(\varepsilon)=\ker(\varepsilon\alpha)$}\\
-&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(4.7)

During this section, it will be convenient in some circumstances to omit singleton blocks in the tabular notation for partitions. For example, we write
\alpha=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{1}&\cdots&B_{r}&D_{1}&\cdots&D_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
to indicate that the upper non-transversals of
\alpha
are all singletons.
As concrete examples, the partitions
\alpha
,
\beta
and
\gamma
in (
4.4
) can be denoted as
\alpha=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1,2\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
,
\beta=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1,2\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
and
\gamma=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
.

In the remainder of this section, an important role will be played by the map

P\to M:\varepsilon=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\mapsto\bar{\varepsilon}=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1&\cdots&r&\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr).

(4.8)

Here as usual we assume that
\min(A_{1})<\cdots<\min(A_{r})
, which ensures that
\bar{\varepsilon}
is planar whenever
\varepsilon
is. Keeping in mind the convention regarding singleton blocks, we note that
\ker(\bar{\varepsilon})=\Delta_{{\bf n}}
for all
\varepsilon\in P
.
Because of the identity
\varepsilon=\bar{\varepsilon}^{*}\bar{\varepsilon}
, the map
\varepsilon\mapsto\bar{\varepsilon}
is injective.
It is worth noting that this map need not map projections to projections. In fact, one can check that
\bar{\varepsilon}
is a projection if and only if
\bar{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon
is a partial identity, i.e. has the form
\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil a_{1}&\cdots&a_{r}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil a_{1}&\cdots&a_{r}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
for some
a_{1},\ldots,a_{r}\in{\bf n}
.

Theorem 4.9
.

Let
n\geq 2
, let
M
be any of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
,
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{n}
,
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}
or
\mathcal{M}_{n}
, and let
\mu:Q^{-}\times M\to Q^{-}
be the action in (
4.7
). Then
\mu
is faithful and monogenic, and consequently

\deg(M)\leq\deg_{\operatorname{rc}}(M)\leq 1+|Q|.

Proof.

As explained above, the minimal congruences of
M
are
(\zeta,\alpha)^{\sharp}
,
(\zeta,\beta)^{\sharp}
and
(\zeta,\gamma)^{\sharp}
, where
\zeta,\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in M
are as in (
4.4
). Thus, by Lemma
3.4
we can establish faithfulness of
\mu
by showing that it separates each of
(\zeta,\alpha)
,
(\zeta,\beta)
and
(\zeta,\gamma)
. For this, we define

Given Lemma
3.2
, it remains to check that
\mu
is monogenic, and for this we claim that
Q^{-}=\langle\pi\rangle_{\mu}
, where
\pi\in Q
is as above. We have already observed that
-=\mu(\pi,\alpha)
, and for
\varepsilon\in Q
it is easy to check that
\varepsilon=\mu(\pi,\bar{\varepsilon})
, where
\bar{\varepsilon}
is as in (
4.8
).
∎

Remark 4.10
.

If instead we took
{\preceq}={\leq_{\mathscr{R}}}
and
Q=P
, we would obtain the alternative action
{P^{-}\times M\to P^{-}}
, as in Remark
3.13
(i)
, which we will here denote by
\mu^{\prime}
. It turns out that
\mu^{\prime}
is not faithful for any of the diagram monoids in Theorem
4.9
. Indeed, for any
\alpha\in M
with
\operatorname{rank}(\alpha)=0
, and for
\varepsilon\in Q^{-}
, one can check that

\mu^{\prime}(\varepsilon,\alpha)=\begin{cases}\alpha^{*}\alpha&\text{if $\varepsilon\in P_{0}$}\\
-&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Combining this with (
2.3
), it follows that
(\alpha,\beta)\in\ker(\mu^{\prime})
if
\alpha\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}\beta
. This is to say that
\lambda\subseteq\ker(\mu^{\prime})
, where
\lambda
is the congruence of
M
in (
4.2
).
On the other hand, with
\beta,\gamma,\zeta\in M
as in (
4.4
), we have

\mu^{\prime}(\zeta,\zeta)=\zeta,\hskip 20.00003pt\mu^{\prime}(\zeta,\beta)={\lower 6.02773pt\hbox{
\hbox to46.09pt{\vbox to20.49pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-6.82875pt\lower-1.70715pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 70.87 0 M 73.23 0 C 73.23 1.3 72.17 2.36 70.87 2.36 C 69.56 2.36 68.5 1.3 68.5 0 C 68.5 -1.3 69.56 -2.36 70.87 -2.36 C 72.17 -2.36 73.23 -1.3 73.23 0 Z M 70.87 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}},\hskip 20.00003pt\mu^{\prime}(\gamma,\zeta)=-\and\mu^{\prime}(\gamma,\gamma)=\gamma,

which shows that neither
(\zeta,\beta)
nor
(\zeta,\gamma)
belongs to
\ker(\mu^{\prime})
. Since
(\zeta,\beta)\in\rho
and
(\zeta,\gamma)\in\eta
, it follows that
\rho\not\subseteq\ker(\mu^{\prime})
and
\eta\not\subseteq\ker(\mu^{\prime})
. Thus, keeping the structure of the congruence lattice
\operatorname{Cong}(M)
in mind (see Figure
4
), it follows that in fact
\ker(\mu^{\prime})=\lambda
.

4.2
Lower bound

We continue to fix the monoid
M
, being one of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
,
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{n}
,
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}
or
\mathcal{M}_{n}
, with
n\geq 2
. Our goal now is to show that
1+|Q|
is a lower bound for
\deg(M)
, thus completing the proof of Theorem
4.1
.

In what follows, we fix the elements
\zeta,\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in M
from (
4.4
), as well as the map
{P=P(M)\to M:\varepsilon\mapsto\bar{\varepsilon}}
from (
4.8
). For
X\subseteq P
we write
\bar{X}=\{{\bar{\varepsilon}}:{\varepsilon\in X}\}
, noting that
|\bar{X}|=|X|
, as
\varepsilon\mapsto\bar{\varepsilon}
is injective. Also recall that
P_{r}=\{{\varepsilon\in P}:{\operatorname{rank}(\varepsilon)=r}\}
denotes the set of projections of rank
0\leq r\leq n
.

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
\sigma
be a right congruence of
M
.

(i)

If
\sigma
separates
\{\zeta,\alpha\}
, then it separates
\bar{P}_{2}
.

(ii)

If
\sigma
separates
\{\zeta,\beta\}
, then it separates
\bar{P}_{0}
.

(iii)

If
\sigma
separates
\{\zeta,\gamma\}
, then it separates
\bar{P}_{1}
.

Proof.

(i)
.
Aiming to prove the contrapositive, suppose
(\bar{\varepsilon}_{1},\bar{\varepsilon}_{2})\in\sigma
for distinct
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\in P_{2}
, and write

\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1&2&\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A&B&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1&2&\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil D&E&F_{1}&\cdots&F_{t}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr).

(4.12)

We must show that
(\zeta,\alpha)\in\sigma
.

Case 1.
Suppose first that
A\cup B\not=D\cup E
. Without loss of generality, we can fix some
a\in A
and
b\in B
such that at least one of
a,b
does not belong to
D\cup E
. Then with
\theta=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil a,b\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\in M
, we have
(\alpha,\zeta)=(\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}\theta,\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}\theta)\in\sigma
.

Case 2.
Next suppose
A\cup B=D\cup E
, but
A\not=D
(and
B\not=E
). (Since
\min(A)<\min(B)
and
\min(D)<\min(E)
, it is impossible to have
A=E
and
B=D
.) Without loss of generality, we can fix some
a\in A
and
b\in B
such that
a,b\in D
or
a,b\in E
. Then with
\theta=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil a,b\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\in M
, we again have
(\alpha,\zeta)=(\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}\theta,\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}\theta)\in\sigma
.

Case 3.
Finally, suppose
A=D
and
B=E
. Since
\varepsilon_{1}\not=\varepsilon_{2}
, we can assume without loss of generality that there exist
x,y\in C_{1}
such that
x\in F_{1}
and
y\in F_{2}
. Also let
a=\min(A)
and
b=\min(B)
, noting that
a<b
. Then with
\theta=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil a,x&b,y\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
or
\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil a,y&b,x\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
, whichever is planar (and hence belongs to
M
), we again have
(\alpha,\zeta)=(\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}\theta,\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}\theta)\in\sigma
.

(ii)
. Suppose
(\bar{\varepsilon}_{1},\bar{\varepsilon}_{2})\in\sigma
for distinct
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\in P_{0}
; this time we must show that
(\zeta,\beta)\in\sigma
. Without loss of generality, we can fix some
(x,y)\in\operatorname{coker}(\varepsilon_{1})\setminus\operatorname{coker}(\varepsilon_{2})
, say with
x<y
. Then with
\theta=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil x&y\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1&2\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\in M
we have
(\beta,\zeta)=(\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}\theta,\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}\theta)\in\sigma
.

(iii)
.
Suppose
(\bar{\varepsilon}_{1},\bar{\varepsilon}_{2})\in\sigma
for distinct
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\in P_{1}
, and write

\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1&\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A&B_{1}&\cdots&B_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1&\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil C&D_{1}&\cdots&D_{t}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr).

(4.13)

Case 1.
Suppose first that
A\not=C
. Without loss of generality, we can fix some
a\in A\setminus C
. Then with
\theta=\binom{a}{1}\in M
we have
(\gamma,\zeta)=(\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}\theta,\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}\theta)\in\sigma
.

Case 2.
Now suppose
A=C
, and fix some
a\in A
. Without loss of generality, we can also fix some
{(x,y)\in\operatorname{coker}(\varepsilon_{1})\setminus\operatorname{coker}(\varepsilon_{2})}
. Then with
\theta=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil x&a,y\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1&\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
or
\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil y&a,x\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1&\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
, whichever is planar, we have
(\gamma,\zeta)=(\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}\theta,\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}\theta)\in\sigma
.
∎

Here then is the last part of the proof of Theorem
4.1
:

Proposition 4.14
.

If
n\geq 2
, and if
M
is any of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
,
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{n}
,
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}
or
\mathcal{M}_{n}
, then

\deg(M)\geq 1+|Q|.

Proof.

Let
\mu:X\times M\to X
be a faithful action. We prove the result by showing that
{|X|\geq 1+|Q|}
. Throughout the proof it will be convenient to write
x\delta=\mu(x,\delta)
for
x\in X
and
\delta\in M
.

Since
\mu
is faithful, it follows from Lemma
3.4
and (
4.3
) that it separates the pairs
(\zeta,\alpha)
,
(\zeta,\beta)
and
(\zeta,\gamma)
. Thus, we can fix elements
{x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}\in X}
for which

x_{0}\zeta\not=x_{0}\beta,\hskip 20.00003ptx_{1}\zeta\not=x_{1}\gamma\and x_{2}\zeta\not=x_{2}\alpha.

(4.15)

We note that
x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}
need not be distinct. We also define the sets

Y_{i}=x_{i}\bar{P}_{i}=\{{x_{i}\bar{\varepsilon}}:{\varepsilon\in P_{i}}\}\subseteq X\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $i=0,1,2$.}

By Lemmas
3.5
and
4.11
, each map
P_{i}\to Y_{i}:\varepsilon\mapsto x_{i}\bar{\varepsilon}
is a bijection, and hence

|Y_{i}|=|P_{i}|\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $i=0,1,2$.}

(4.16)

Our strategy for obtaining
|X|\geq 1+|Q|
involves showing that

|Y_{0}\cup Y_{1}\cup Y_{2}|=|Q|=|P_{0}|+|P_{1}|+|P_{2}|,

and that
X\setminus(Y_{0}\cup Y_{1}\cup Y_{2})
is non-empty.

We begin by claiming that

(Y_{1}\cup Y_{2})\cap\{{x\zeta}:{x\in X}\}=\varnothing.

(4.17)

To prove this, suppose to the contrary that
x_{i}\bar{\varepsilon}=x\zeta
for some
\varepsilon\in P_{i}
and
x\in X
, where
i=1
or
2
. Since
\zeta=\zeta\bar{\varepsilon}^{*}
, it then follows that

x\zeta=x\zeta\bar{\varepsilon}^{*}=x_{i}\bar{\varepsilon}\bar{\varepsilon}^{*}=\begin{cases}x_{1}\gamma&\text{if $i=1$}\\
x_{2}\pi&\text{if $i=2$,}\end{cases}

where again
\pi={\lower 6.02773pt\hbox{
\hbox to46.09pt{\vbox to20.49pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-6.82875pt\lower-1.70715pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 11.81 23.62 M 14.17 23.62 C 14.17 24.93 13.12 25.98 11.81 25.98 C 10.51 25.98 9.45 24.93 9.45 23.62 C 9.45 22.32 10.51 21.26 11.81 21.26 C 13.12 21.26 14.17 22.32 14.17 23.62 Z M 11.81 23.62}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 23.62 23.62 M 25.98 23.62 C 25.98 24.93 24.93 25.98 23.62 25.98 C 22.32 25.98 21.26 24.93 21.26 23.62 C 21.26 22.32 22.32 21.26 23.62 21.26 C 24.93 21.26 25.98 22.32 25.98 23.62 Z M 23.62 23.62}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 35.43 23.62 M 37.8 23.62 C 37.8 24.93 36.74 25.98 35.43 25.98 C 34.13 25.98 33.07 24.93 33.07 23.62 C 33.07 22.32 34.13 21.26 35.43 21.26 C 36.74 21.26 37.8 22.32 37.8 23.62 Z M 35.43 23.62}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 70.87 23.62 M 73.23 23.62 C 73.23 24.93 72.17 25.98 70.87 25.98 C 69.56 25.98 68.5 24.93 68.5 23.62 C 68.5 22.32 69.56 21.26 70.87 21.26 C 72.17 21.26 73.23 22.32 73.23 23.62 Z M 70.87 23.62}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 11.81 0 M 14.17 0 C 14.17 1.3 13.12 2.36 11.81 2.36 C 10.51 2.36 9.45 1.3 9.45 0 C 9.45 -1.3 10.51 -2.36 11.81 -2.36 C 13.12 -2.36 14.17 -1.3 14.17 0 Z M 11.81 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 23.62 0 M 25.98 0 C 25.98 1.3 24.93 2.36 23.62 2.36 C 22.32 2.36 21.26 1.3 21.26 0 C 21.26 -1.3 22.32 -2.36 23.62 -2.36 C 24.93 -2.36 25.98 -1.3 25.98 0 Z M 23.62 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 35.43 0 M 37.8 0 C 37.8 1.3 36.74 2.36 35.43 2.36 C 34.13 2.36 33.07 1.3 33.07 0 C 33.07 -1.3 34.13 -2.36 35.43 -2.36 C 36.74 -2.36 37.8 -1.3 37.8 0 Z M 35.43 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 70.87 0 M 73.23 0 C 73.23 1.3 72.17 2.36 70.87 2.36 C 69.56 2.36 68.5 1.3 68.5 0 C 68.5 -1.3 69.56 -2.36 70.87 -2.36 C 72.17 -2.36 73.23 -1.3 73.23 0 Z M 70.87 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 11.81 23.62 L 11.81 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 23.62 23.62 L 23.62 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{0.4pt,2.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray={0.4pt,2.0pt},stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 41.34 23.62 L 64.96 23.62}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{0.4pt,2.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray={0.4pt,2.0pt},stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 41.34 0 L 64.96 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}}
. The
i=1
and
i=2
cases lead respectively to

x_{1}\zeta=x_{1}\gamma\zeta=x\zeta\zeta=x\zeta=x_{1}\gamma\hskip 20.00003pt\text{or}\hskip 20.00003ptx_{2}\zeta=x_{2}\pi\zeta=x\zeta\zeta=x\zeta=x\zeta\alpha=x_{2}\pi\alpha=x_{2}\alpha,

both contradicting (
4.15
).

Now that we have proved (
4.17
), our next claim is that

Y_{1}\cap Y_{2}=\varnothing.

(4.18)

To prove this, suppose to the contrary that
x_{1}\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}=x_{2}\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}
for some
\varepsilon_{1}\in P_{1}
and
\varepsilon_{2}\in P_{2}
. By considering the form of
\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}
and
\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}
, one can check that
\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}^{*}
is equal to one of

{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 35.43 0 M 37.8 0 C 37.8 1.3 36.74 2.36 35.43 2.36 C 34.13 2.36 33.07 1.3 33.07 0 C 33.07 -1.3 34.13 -2.36 35.43 -2.36 C 36.74 -2.36 37.8 -1.3 37.8 0 Z M 35.43 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 70.87 0 M 73.23 0 C 73.23 1.3 72.17 2.36 70.87 2.36 C 69.56 2.36 68.5 1.3 68.5 0 C 68.5 -1.3 69.56 -2.36 70.87 -2.36 C 72.17 -2.36 73.23 -1.3 73.23 0 Z M 70.87 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{}{}{}{}}}}{}
{}
{}
{}
{}{}{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{}{}{}{}}}}{}
{}
{}
{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 11.81 0 C 11.81 2.61 13.93 4.72 16.54 4.72 M 16.54 4.72 L 18.9 4.72 M 18.9 4.72 C 21.51 4.72 23.62 2.61 23.62 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{0.4pt,2.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray={0.4pt,2.0pt},stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 41.34 23.62 L 64.96 23.62}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{0.4pt,2.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray={0.4pt,2.0pt},stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 41.34 0 L 64.96 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}}.

In any case, it follows that
\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}^{*}\alpha=\zeta
. On the other hand, we have
\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}^{*}\alpha=\pi\alpha=\alpha
. Combining these, we obtain

x_{1}\zeta=(x_{1}\bar{\varepsilon}_{1})\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}^{*}\alpha=(x_{2}\bar{\varepsilon}_{2})\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}^{*}\alpha=x_{2}\alpha.

From
\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}\zeta=\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}\zeta=\zeta
, we also have

x_{1}\zeta=(x_{1}\bar{\varepsilon}_{1})\zeta=(x_{2}\bar{\varepsilon}_{2})\zeta=x_{2}\zeta.

Combining the last two conclusions, it follows that
x_{2}\alpha=x_{2}\zeta
, which contradicts (
4.15
).

Now that we have proved (
4.18
), our next claim is that

(Y_{1}\cup Y_{2})\cap Y_{0}=\varnothing.

(4.19)

To prove this, suppose to the contrary that
x_{i}\bar{\varepsilon}_{i}=x_{0}\bar{\varepsilon}_{0}
for some
\varepsilon_{0}\in P_{0}
and
\varepsilon_{i}\in P_{i}
, where
i=1
or
2
. We then have
x_{i}\bar{\varepsilon}_{i}\bar{\varepsilon}_{i}^{*}=x_{0}\bar{\varepsilon}_{0}\bar{\varepsilon}_{i}^{*}
. Now,

\bar{\varepsilon}_{i}\bar{\varepsilon}_{i}^{*}=\gamma\text{ or }\pi\text{ (for $i=1$ or $2$, respectively)}\and\bar{\varepsilon}_{0}\bar{\varepsilon}_{i}^{*}=\zeta\text{ or }\beta,

but we note that
\bar{\varepsilon}_{0}\bar{\varepsilon}_{i}^{*}=\beta
is only possible when
i=2
. We see then that one of the following holds:

x_{1}\gamma=x_{0}\zeta,\hskip 20.00003ptx_{2}\pi=x_{0}\zeta\hskip 20.00003pt\text{or}\hskip 20.00003ptx_{2}\pi=x_{0}\beta.

Keeping in mind
\gamma=\bar{\gamma}
and
\pi=\bar{\pi}
, the first two options contradict (
4.17
), so suppose instead that
x_{2}\pi=x_{0}\beta
. As noted above, this case arises when
i=2
and
\bar{\varepsilon}_{0}\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}^{*}=\beta
, and so our original assumption was that
x_{2}\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}=x_{0}\bar{\varepsilon}_{0}
. Putting this all together, we have

x_{2}\zeta=x_{2}\pi\zeta=x_{0}\beta\zeta=x_{0}\zeta=(x_{0}\bar{\varepsilon}_{0})\bar{\varepsilon}_{0}^{*}=(x_{2}\bar{\varepsilon}_{2})\bar{\varepsilon}_{0}^{*}=x_{2}(\bar{\varepsilon}_{0}\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}^{*})^{*}=x_{2}\beta^{*}=x_{2}\alpha,

which contradicts (
4.15
). This completes the proof of (
4.19
).

Combining (
4.18
) and (
4.19
), we see that
Y_{0}
,
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
are pairwise disjoint. Given (
4.16
), it follows that the subset
Y_{0}\cup Y_{1}\cup Y_{2}
of
X
has size
|P_{0}|+|P_{1}|+|P_{2}|=|Q|
. Thus, it remains only to show that

X\setminus(Y_{0}\cup Y_{1}\cup Y_{2})\not=\varnothing.

(4.20)

This is certainly true if either of
x_{1}\zeta
or
x_{2}\zeta
does not belong to
Y_{0}\cup Y_{1}\cup Y_{2}
. Given (
4.17
), the only alternative is that
x_{1}\zeta
and
x_{2}\zeta
both belong to
Y_{0}
, so we now assume that this is the case. Thus, for
i=1,2
we have
x_{i}\zeta=x_{0}\bar{\varepsilon}_{i}
for some
\varepsilon_{i}\in P_{0}
, and then
x_{i}\zeta=x_{i}\zeta\zeta=x_{0}\bar{\varepsilon}_{i}\zeta=x_{0}\zeta
, so in fact

x_{0}\zeta=x_{1}\zeta=x_{2}\zeta.

(4.21)

We can complete the proof of (
4.20
), and hence of the proposition, by showing that

x_{2}\alpha\not\in Y_{0}\cup Y_{1}\cup Y_{2}.

To do so, suppose to the contrary that
x_{2}\alpha\in Y_{i}
for some
i=0,1,2
, so that
x_{2}\alpha=x_{i}\bar{\varepsilon}
for some
\varepsilon\in P_{i}
. Keeping (
4.21
) in mind, we then have

x_{2}\alpha=x_{2}\alpha\zeta=x_{i}\bar{\varepsilon}\zeta=x_{i}\zeta=x_{2}\zeta,

contradicting (
4.15
). As noted above, this completes the proof.
∎

5
The Temperley–Lieb monoid

In this section we consider the Temperley–Lieb monoid
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
, our main result being the following:

Theorem 5.1
.

For
n\geq 3
we have

\deg^{\prime}(\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n})=|Q|\and\deg(\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n})=\deg_{\operatorname{rc}}(\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n})=1+|Q|,

where

Q=\begin{cases}P_{0}\cup P_{2}\cup P_{4}&\text{if $n$ is even}\\
P_{1}\cup P_{3}&\text{if $n$ is odd.}\end{cases}

A formula for
|Q|
can be found in Proposition
7.8
.

Here as usual
P_{r}=\{{\varepsilon\in P(\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n})}:{\operatorname{rank}(\varepsilon)=r}\}
for
0\leq r\leq n
, but we note that this set is non-empty precisely when
r\equiv n\pmod{2}
.
The even case of Theorem
5.1
in fact follows from Theorem
4.1
, and the previously-mentioned isomorphism
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}\cong\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{2n}
, which maps
{P_{r}(\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n})\to P_{2r}(\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{2n})}
; see Figure
3
.

We are therefore left to deal with the odd case of Theorem
5.1
. For this it is again convenient to work with an isomorphic copy of
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{2n-1}
. Specifically, it was explained in
[
28
, Section 1]
that
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{2n-1}
is isomorphic to the monoid

M=\{{\alpha\in\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}}:{1\text{ and }1^{\prime}\text{ belong to the same block of }\alpha}\}.

Indeed, the isomorphism
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}\to\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{2n}
maps an element
\alpha\in M
to a Temperley–Lieb diagram
\widetilde{\alpha}\in\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{2n}
with transversal
\{1,1^{\prime}\}
, the set of which is clearly isomorphic to
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{2n-1}
; again see Figure
3
.

We fix the above monoid
M(\cong\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{2n-1})
for the remainder of the section. We also write

P=P(M)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptQ=P_{1}\cup P_{2},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{where}\hskip 10.00002ptP_{r}=\{{\varepsilon\in P}:{\operatorname{rank}(\varepsilon)=r}\}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $1\leq r\leq n$.}

(Because of its defining property,
M
contains no partitions of rank
0
, but it contains partitions of every rank in
\{1,\ldots,n\}
, regardless of parity.) We therefore wish to prove the following:

Theorem 5.2
.

For
n\geq 2
we have

\deg^{\prime}(M)=|Q|\and\deg(M)=\deg_{\operatorname{rc}}(M)=1+|Q|,\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003ptQ=P_{1}\cup P_{2}.

Our strategy for proving this is similar to that for Theorem
4.1
. We will therefore be somewhat briefer.

As before, we need to understand the minimal congruences of
M
. Since
M\cong\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{2n-1}
, it follows from
[
23
, Theorem 9.1]
that there are two of these, which we again denote by

\displaystyle\lambda

\displaystyle=\Delta_{M}\cup\{{(\alpha,\beta)\in M\times M}:{\operatorname{rank}(\alpha)=\operatorname{rank}(\beta)=1,\ \alpha\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}\beta}\},

\displaystyle\rho

\displaystyle=\Delta_{M}\cup\{{(\alpha,\beta)\in M\times M}:{\operatorname{rank}(\alpha)=\operatorname{rank}(\beta)=1,\ \alpha\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}\beta}\}.

We also have
\lambda=(\zeta,\alpha)^{\sharp}
and
\rho=(\zeta,\beta)^{\sharp}
, where

\zeta={\lower 6.02773pt\hbox{
\hbox to46.09pt{\vbox to20.49pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-6.82875pt\lower-1.70715pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

(5.3)

We still have the pre-order
\preceq
on
M
, as in (
4.6
), and its associated equivalence
{\approx}={\preceq}\cap{\succeq}
, leading to the representation
\mu:Q^{-}\times M\to Q^{-}
, as in (
4.7
). In the proofs that follow we make use of the fact that the map
\varepsilon\mapsto\bar{\varepsilon}
from (
4.8
) still maps
P
into
M
.

are distinct. Monogenicity follows from the fact that
