Lemma 5.5

(cf. Lemma
4.11
)
.

Let
\sigma
be a right congruence of
M
.

(i)

If
\sigma
separates
\{\zeta,\alpha\}
, then it separates
\bar{P}_{2}
.

(ii)

If
\sigma
separates
\{\zeta,\beta\}
, then it separates
\bar{P}_{1}
.

Proof.

(i)
. Suppose
(\bar{\varepsilon}_{1},\bar{\varepsilon}_{2})\in\sigma
for distinct
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\in P_{2}
, and write
\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}
and
\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}
as in (
4.12
), noting that
1\in A<B
and
1\in D<E
. We must show that
(\zeta,\alpha)\in\sigma
.

Case 1.
Suppose first that
A\not=D
. Without loss of generality, fix some
a\in A\setminus D
, and let
b\in B
be arbitrary, noting that
1<a<b
. It follows that
\theta=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1&a,b\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1&\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\in M
. We then have
(\alpha,\zeta)=(\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}\theta,\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}\theta)\in\sigma
. (Note that by the form of
\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}
and
\theta
, the product
\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}\theta
could only be equal to
\alpha
or
\zeta
. It could only be equal to
\alpha
if the edge
\{a,b\}
of
\theta
connected
D
and
E
. Since
a\not\in D
, this could only be the case if
a\in E
and
b\in D
, but this is impossible since
a<b
and
D<E
.)

Case 2.
Next suppose
A=D
but
B\not=E
, and without loss of generality, fix
b\in B\setminus E
. Then with
\theta=\binom{1,b}{1}\in M
, we have
(\alpha,\zeta)=(\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}\theta,\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}\theta)\in\sigma
.

Case 3.
Finally, suppose
A=D
and
B=E
, and without loss of generality fix some
{(x,y)\in\operatorname{coker}(\varepsilon_{1})\setminus\operatorname{coker}(\varepsilon_{2})}
. We assume that
x<y
, and we also let
b\in B
be arbitrary. By planarity of
\varepsilon_{1}
, we either have
1<x<y<b
or
1<b<x<y
. We then define
\theta=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1,x&b,y\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1&\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
or
\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1,y&b,x\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1&\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
, respectively, and we have
\theta\in M
, and
(\alpha,\zeta)=(\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}\theta,\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}\theta)\in\sigma
.

(ii)
. Suppose
(\bar{\varepsilon}_{1},\bar{\varepsilon}_{2})\in\sigma
for distinct
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\in P_{1}
, and write
\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}
and
\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}
as in (
4.13
).

Case 1.
Suppose first that
A\not=C
, and without loss of generality fix some
a\in A\setminus C
, noting that
a\not=1
. Then with
\theta=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1&a\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1&2\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\in M
we have
(\beta,\zeta)=(\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}\theta,\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}\theta)\in\sigma
.

Case 2.
Now suppose
A=C
, and without loss of generality fix some
(x,y)\in\operatorname{coker}(\varepsilon_{1})\setminus\operatorname{coker}(\varepsilon_{2})
with
x<y
. Then with
\theta=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1,x&y\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1&2\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\in M
we have
(\beta,\zeta)=(\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}\theta,\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}\theta)\in\sigma
.
∎

The next result completes the proof of Theorem
5.2
, and hence of Theorem
5.1
.

Proposition 5.6

(cf. Proposition
4.14
)
.

If
n\geq 2
, then
\deg(M)\geq 1+|Q|
.

Proof.

Let
\mu:X\times M\to X
be a faithful action, denoted
\mu(x,\delta)=x\delta
, as in the proof of Proposition
4.14
. We must show that
|X|\geq 1+|Q|
. Since
\mu
is faithful, we can fix elements
x_{1},x_{2}\in X
such that

x_{1}\zeta\not=x_{1}\beta\and x_{2}\zeta\not=x_{2}\alpha.

(5.7)

We define the sets
Y_{i}=x_{i}\bar{P}_{i}=\{{x_{i}\bar{\varepsilon}}:{\varepsilon\in P_{i}}\}
for
i=1,2
, noting that
|Y_{i}|=|P_{i}|
by Lemmas
3.5
and
5.5
.

We first claim that

Y_{2}\cap\{{x\zeta}:{x\in X}\}=\varnothing.

(5.8)

Indeed, suppose to the contrary that
x_{2}\bar{\varepsilon}=x\zeta
for some
\varepsilon\in P_{2}
and
x\in X
. Again writing
{\pi={\lower 6.02773pt\hbox{
\hbox to46.09pt{\vbox to20.49pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-6.82875pt\lower-1.70715pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 11.81 23.62 M 14.17 23.62 C 14.17 24.93 13.12 25.98 11.81 25.98 C 10.51 25.98 9.45 24.93 9.45 23.62 C 9.45 22.32 10.51 21.26 11.81 21.26 C 13.12 21.26 14.17 22.32 14.17 23.62 Z M 11.81 23.62}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 23.62 23.62 M 25.98 23.62 C 25.98 24.93 24.93 25.98 23.62 25.98 C 22.32 25.98 21.26 24.93 21.26 23.62 C 21.26 22.32 22.32 21.26 23.62 21.26 C 24.93 21.26 25.98 22.32 25.98 23.62 Z M 23.62 23.62}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 35.43 23.62 M 37.8 23.62 C 37.8 24.93 36.74 25.98 35.43 25.98 C 34.13 25.98 33.07 24.93 33.07 23.62 C 33.07 22.32 34.13 21.26 35.43 21.26 C 36.74 21.26 37.8 22.32 37.8 23.62 Z M 35.43 23.62}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 11.81 0 M 14.17 0 C 14.17 1.3 13.12 2.36 11.81 2.36 C 10.51 2.36 9.45 1.3 9.45 0 C 9.45 -1.3 10.51 -2.36 11.81 -2.36 C 13.12 -2.36 14.17 -1.3 14.17 0 Z M 11.81 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 23.62 0 M 25.98 0 C 25.98 1.3 24.93 2.36 23.62 2.36 C 22.32 2.36 21.26 1.3 21.26 0 C 21.26 -1.3 22.32 -2.36 23.62 -2.36 C 24.93 -2.36 25.98 -1.3 25.98 0 Z M 23.62 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 35.43 0 M 37.8 0 C 37.8 1.3 36.74 2.36 35.43 2.36 C 34.13 2.36 33.07 1.3 33.07 0 C 33.07 -1.3 34.13 -2.36 35.43 -2.36 C 36.74 -2.36 37.8 -1.3 37.8 0 Z M 35.43 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 70.87 0 M 73.23 0 C 73.23 1.3 72.17 2.36 70.87 2.36 C 69.56 2.36 68.5 1.3 68.5 0 C 68.5 -1.3 69.56 -2.36 70.87 -2.36 C 72.17 -2.36 73.23 -1.3 73.23 0 Z M 70.87 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 11.81 23.62 L 11.81 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 23.62 23.62 L 23.62 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{0.4pt,2.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray={0.4pt,2.0pt},stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 41.34 23.62 L 64.96 23.62}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{0.4pt,2.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray={0.4pt,2.0pt},stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 41.34 0 L 64.96 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}}}
, we have
x_{2}\pi=x_{2}\bar{\varepsilon}\bar{\varepsilon}^{*}=x\zeta\bar{\varepsilon}^{*}=x\zeta
. It follows that

x_{2}\zeta=x_{2}\pi\zeta=x\zeta\zeta=x\zeta=x\zeta\alpha=x_{2}\pi\alpha=x_{2}\alpha,

contradicting (
5.7
).

Next we claim that

Y_{1}\cap Y_{2}=\varnothing.

(5.9)

Indeed, suppose to the contrary that
x_{1}\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}=x_{2}\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}
for some
\varepsilon_{1}\in P_{1}
and
\varepsilon_{2}\in P_{2}
. Then

x_{2}\pi=x_{2}\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}^{*}=x_{1}\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}^{*}.

Noting that
\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}^{*}=\zeta
or
\beta
, it follows that
x_{2}\pi=x_{1}\zeta
or
x_{2}\pi=x_{1}\beta
. The first contradicts (
5.8
), so suppose instead that
x_{2}\pi=x_{1}\beta
. This occurs when
\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}^{*}=\beta
, from which it follows that
\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}^{*}=\beta^{*}=\alpha
. We then calculate

x_{1}\zeta=x_{1}\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}^{*}=x_{2}\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}^{*}=x_{2}\alpha\and x_{1}\zeta=x_{1}\beta\zeta=x_{2}\pi\zeta=x_{2}\zeta,

so that
x_{2}\alpha=x_{2}\zeta
, contradicting (
5.7
).

Now that we have proved (
5.9
), it follows that
|Y_{1}\cup Y_{2}|=|Y_{1}|+|Y_{2}|=|P_{1}|+|P_{2}|=|Q|
, so it remains to show that

X\setminus(Y_{1}\cup Y_{2})\not=\varnothing.

(5.10)

This is certainly true if
x_{2}\zeta\not\in Y_{1}\cup Y_{2}
, so suppose this is not the case. It follows from (
5.8
) that
x_{2}\zeta\in Y_{1}
, so we have
x_{2}\zeta=x_{1}\bar{\varepsilon}
for some
\varepsilon\in P_{1}
. We then have
x_{2}\zeta=x_{2}\zeta\zeta=x_{1}\bar{\varepsilon}\zeta=x_{1}\zeta
. We will complete the proof of (
5.10
), and hence of the proposition, by showing that

x_{2}\alpha\not\in Y_{1}\cup Y_{2}.

To do so, suppose to the contrary that
x_{2}\alpha=x_{i}\bar{\varepsilon}_{i}
for some
\varepsilon_{i}\in P_{i}
, where
i=1
or
2
. Then

x_{2}\alpha=x_{2}\alpha\zeta=x_{i}\bar{\varepsilon}_{i}\zeta=x_{i}\zeta=x_{2}\zeta,

using
x_{1}\zeta=x_{2}\zeta
in the last step. This again contradicts (
5.7
).
∎

6
The Brauer monoid

We now come to our last diagram monoid, the Brauer monoid
\mathcal{B}_{n}
. Our main result here is the following, stated in terms of the projection sets
P_{r}=\{{\varepsilon\in P(\mathcal{B}_{n})}:{\operatorname{rank}(\varepsilon)=r}\}
:

Theorem 6.1
.

For
n\geq 3
we have

\deg^{\prime}(\mathcal{B}_{n})=\begin{cases}|P_{1}|+3|P_{3}|&\text{if $n$ is odd}\\
|P_{0}|+2|P_{2}|+3|P_{4}|&\text{if $n$ is even,}\end{cases}\and\deg(\mathcal{B}_{n})=1+\deg^{\prime}(\mathcal{B}_{n}).

If
n
is odd, then
\deg(\mathcal{B}_{n})=\deg_{\operatorname{rc}}(\mathcal{B}_{n})
.

It was shown in
[
21
, Theorem 8.4]
that
|P_{r}|=\tbinom{n}{r}(n-r-1)!!
. Here as usual for a positive odd integer
m
we define the double factorial
{m!!=m(m-2)(m-4)\cdots 1}
, and by convention
(-1)!!=1
. One can then easily check that Theorem
6.1
leads to the explicit formulae

\deg^{\prime}(\mathcal{B}_{n})=\begin{cases}\frac{n+1}{2}\cdot n!!&\text{if $n\geq 3$ is odd}\\
\frac{(n+4)(n+2)}{8}\cdot(n-1)!!&\text{if $n\geq 4$ is even.}\end{cases}

(6.2)

Remark 6.3
.

As we will see, the methods we use here are necessarily rather different from those of previous sections. For one thing, as indicated by the theorem itself, we generally have the strict inequality
\deg(\mathcal{B}_{n})<\deg_{\operatorname{rc}}(\mathcal{B}_{n})
for even
n\geq 4
.
For example, it follows from the theorem that
\deg(\mathcal{B}_{4})=19
, and from GAP computations
[
26
,
48
]
that
\deg_{\operatorname{rc}}(\mathcal{B}_{4})=22
.
Another dissimilarity is that
\deg^{\prime}(\mathcal{B}_{n})
is not simply a sum of
|P_{r}|
parameters, but rather a (non-trivial) linear combination. The reason for this will become clear as we progress.

Our strategy for proving Theorem
6.1
is as follows. After gathering the required preliminaries in Section
6.1
, we build a right congruence
\sigma
in Section
6.2
, using the construction from Section
3.4
. We then use this to show that the claimed value for
\deg(\mathcal{B}_{n})
is an upper bound in Sections
6.3
and
6.4
for odd and even
n
, respectively; see Theorems
6.16
and
6.21
. The even case is more involved, and the faithful action of the desired degree is constructed as a push-out of two smaller actions (only one of which involves
\sigma
). Finally, we show that the claimed value of
\deg(\mathcal{B}_{n})
is a lower bound in Sections
6.5
and
6.6
, again split by parity; see Propositions
6.23
and
6.28
. As usual, the minimum-degree faithful actions we construct have global fixed points, so it follows that
{\deg^{\prime}(\mathcal{B}_{n})=\deg(\mathcal{B}_{n})-1}
.

6.1
Preliminaries

In what follows, we will need the characterisation of Green’s relations on
\mathcal{B}_{n}
from
[
12
, Proposition 3.1]
. For
\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal{B}_{n}
we have

\displaystyle\alpha\leq_{\mathscr{L}}\beta

\displaystyle\ \Leftrightarrow\ \operatorname{coker}(\alpha)\supseteq\operatorname{coker}(\beta),

\displaystyle\alpha\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}\beta

\displaystyle\ \Leftrightarrow\ \operatorname{coker}(\alpha)=\operatorname{coker}(\beta),

\displaystyle\alpha\leq_{\mathscr{R}}\beta

\displaystyle\ \Leftrightarrow\ \ker(\alpha)\supseteq\ker(\beta),

\displaystyle\alpha\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}\beta

\displaystyle\ \Leftrightarrow\ \ker(\alpha)=\ker(\beta),

\displaystyle\alpha\leq_{\mathscr{J}}\beta

\displaystyle\ \Leftrightarrow\ \operatorname{rank}(\alpha)\leq\operatorname{rank}(\beta),

\displaystyle\alpha\mathrel{\mathscr{J}}\beta

\displaystyle\ \Leftrightarrow\ \operatorname{rank}(\alpha)=\operatorname{rank}(\beta),

and of course
\mathscr{D}=\mathscr{J}
as
\mathcal{B}_{n}
is finite. Keeping in mind that the rank of a Brauer partition has the same parity as
n
, it follows that the
\mathscr{D}
-classes and non-empty ideals of
\mathcal{B}_{n}
are the sets

D_{r}=\{{\alpha\in\mathcal{B}_{n}}:{\operatorname{rank}(\alpha)=r}\}\and I_{r}=\{{\alpha\in\mathcal{B}_{n}}:{\operatorname{rank}(\alpha)\leq r}\}

for
0\leq r\leq n
with
r\equiv n
(mod
2
). Note that
I_{r}=D_{r}\cup D_{r-2}\cup D_{r-4}\cup\cdots
.

Remark 6.4
.

It is also worth noting that
\leq_{\mathscr{R}}
is precisely the pre-order
\preceq
used in Section
4
; see (
4.6
). As in Remark
4.10
, it follows that the resulting action of
\mathcal{B}_{n}
on
P^{-}=P\cup\{-\}
is not faithful, hence the need for the different approach we take here.

Throughout this section we write

P=P(\mathcal{B}_{n})=\{{\varepsilon\in\mathcal{B}_{n}}:{\varepsilon^{2}=\varepsilon=\varepsilon^{*}}\}\and P_{r}=P_{r}(\mathcal{B}_{n})=P\cap D_{r}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $0\leq r\leq n$,}

noting that
P_{r}
is non-empty precisely when
r\equiv n
(mod
2
). We also write

p_{r}=|P_{r}|=\tbinom{n}{r}(n-r-1)!!.

As before, we will need to understand the minimal congruences of
\mathcal{B}_{n}
, as described in
[
23
, Section 8]
. For odd
n
, there are two minimal congruences, which we will denote by

\lambda=\Delta_{\mathcal{B}_{n}}\cup\mathscr{L}{\restriction}_{D_{1}}\and\rho=\Delta_{\mathcal{B}_{n}}\cup\mathscr{R}{\restriction}_{D_{1}},

and we have
\lambda=(\zeta,\alpha)^{\sharp}
and
\rho=(\zeta,\beta)^{\sharp}
, where

\zeta={\lower 6.02773pt\hbox{
\hbox to71.7pt{\vbox to20.49pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-6.82875pt\lower-1.70715pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

(6.5)

For even
n
there are three minimal congruences, which we will denote by

\lambda=\Delta_{\mathcal{B}_{n}}\cup\mathscr{L}{\restriction}_{D_{0}},\hskip 20.00003pt\rho=\Delta_{\mathcal{B}_{n}}\cup\mathscr{R}{\restriction}_{D_{0}}\and\eta=\Delta_{\mathcal{B}_{n}}\cup\mathscr{H}{\restriction}_{D_{2}},

and we have
\lambda=(\zeta,\alpha)^{\sharp}
,
\rho=(\zeta,\beta)^{\sharp}
and
\eta=(\gamma,\delta)^{\sharp}
, where

(6.6)

6.2
A right congruence

We will soon have to split our consideration into cases according to the parity of
n
. However, a certain right congruence
\sigma
that is parity-independent will play an important role, and we introduce this here. This will have the form
\sigma=\mathcal{R}_{I}\vee\mathscr{L}^{U}=\nabla_{I}\cup\mathscr{L}^{U}{\restriction}_{K}
, as in Section
3.4
; see (
3.9
).

Throughout this section we assume that
n\geq 3
, and we fix
k=\lfloor n/2\rfloor
, so that
{n=2k}
or
{2k+1}
. We also fix the partition

\zeta={\lower 6.02773pt\hbox{
\hbox to46.09pt{\vbox to20.49pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-6.82875pt\lower-1.70715pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}}.

We write
\kappa=\ker(\zeta)=\operatorname{coker}(\zeta)
, and we denote the non-trivial
\kappa
-classes by

Z_{1}<\cdots<Z_{k},\hskip 20.00003pt\text{so}\hskip 20.00003ptZ_{i}=\begin{cases}\{2i,2i+1\}&\text{if $n$ is odd}\\
\{2i-1,2i\}&\text{if $n$ is even.}\end{cases}

When
n
is even, these are
all
the
\kappa
-classes, but when
n
is odd, the last
\kappa
-class is
\{1\}
.
Define the
\mathscr{R}
-class
R=R_{\zeta}
, and let
T
,
K
and
I=\mathcal{B}_{n}\setminus K
be as in (
3.6
). We then have

R=\{{\alpha\in\mathcal{B}_{n}}:{\ker(\alpha)=\kappa}\},\hskip 10.00002ptK=\{{\alpha\in\mathcal{B}_{n}}:{\ker(\alpha)\subseteq\kappa}\}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptI=\{{\alpha\in\mathcal{B}_{n}}:{\ker(\alpha)\not\subseteq\kappa}\}.

Note that the condition
\ker(\alpha)\subseteq\kappa
says that the non-trivial
\ker(\alpha)
-classes are among the
Z_{i}
.
Since
\mathcal{B}_{n}
is stable (as it is finite), and since
D_{\zeta}
(being
D_{0}
or
D_{1}
) is
\mathscr{H}
-trivial, it follows from Lemma
3.7
(iii)
that

T=\{{\alpha\in\mathcal{B}_{n}}:{\alpha\zeta=\zeta}\}.

This set is not easy to describe concisely, but we will instead work with its sub(semi)group

U=T\cap\mathcal{S}_{n}=\{{\alpha\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}:{\alpha\zeta=\zeta}\}=\{{\alpha\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}:{(\forall i\in{\bf k})(\exists j\in{\bf k})\ Z_{i}\alpha=Z_{j}}\}.

This subgroup of
\mathcal{S}_{n}
is the stabiliser of the partition
{\bf n}/\kappa
, and is (isomorphic to) the wreath product
C_{2}\wr\mathcal{S}_{k}
. In later arguments (in which we will need to vary the underlying parameter
n
), we will also denote this group by
U=\mathcal{G}_{n}
, and we note that
|\mathcal{G}_{n}|=2^{k}k!
.

In what follows, it will be convenient in some circumstances to omit some upper and/or lower non-transversals when using the tabular notation for Brauer partitions, although we always list
all
transversals. Specifically, if we write
\alpha=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil a_{1}&\cdots&a_{r}&A_{1}&\cdots&A_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil b_{1}&\cdots&b_{r}&B_{1}&\cdots&B_{t}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
, and if the unlisted upper vertices are
c_{1}<\cdots<c_{u}
(there must be an even number of these), then we assume the remaining upper blocks of
\alpha
are
\{c_{1},c_{2}\},\{c_{3},c_{4}\},\ldots,\{c_{u-1},c_{u}\}
. A similar statement holds for unlisted lower vertices/blocks.
Thus, for example, the elements
\alpha
and
\beta
in (
6.5
) can be denoted as
\alpha=\binom{3}{1}
and
\beta=\binom{1}{3}
, while
\alpha
in (
6.6
) can be written as either
\alpha=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1,4\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
or
\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 2,3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
.

The coming arguments will also use the mapping

\mathcal{B}_{n}\to K:\alpha=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil a_{1}&\cdots&a_{r}&A_{1}&\cdots&A_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil b_{1}&\cdots&b_{r}&B_{1}&\cdots&B_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\mapsto\bar{\alpha}=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1&\cdots&r&\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil b_{1}&\cdots&b_{r}&B_{1}&\cdots&B_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr).

(6.7)

We assume that
all
the blocks of
\alpha
have been listed here, and as usual we have
a_{1}<\cdots<a_{r}
. The upper non-transversals of
\bar{\alpha}
have been omitted, however, as per the above convention, and these are in fact
Z_{k-s+1},\ldots,Z_{k}
.
Note also that
\bar{\alpha}=\theta\alpha
, where
\theta\in\mathcal{S}_{n}
is any permutation for which:

\text{$i\theta=a_{i}$ for each $i\in{\bf r}$,}\and\text{$Z_{k-s+i}\theta=A_{i}$ for each $i\in{\bf s}$.}

(6.8)

Lemma 6.9
.

For any
\alpha\in K
we have
\alpha\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}^{U}\bar{\alpha}
.

Proof.

Let
\alpha\in K
. Since
\ker(\alpha)\subseteq\kappa
, we can write
\alpha=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil a_{1}&\cdots&a_{r}&Z_{i_{1}}&\cdots&Z_{i_{s}}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil b_{1}&\cdots&b_{r}&B_{1}&\cdots&B_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
. Also writing
{{\bf k}\setminus\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{s}\}}=\{j_{1}<\cdots<j_{t}\}
, we note that:

•

Z_{j_{1}}=\{a_{1},a_{2}\}
,
Z_{j_{2}}=\{a_{3},a_{4}\}
, and so on, when
n
is even, or

•

Z_{j_{1}}=\{a_{2},a_{3}\}
,
Z_{j_{2}}=\{a_{4},a_{5}\}
, and so on, when
n
is odd, in which case we also have
a_{1}=1
.

It follows that any permutation
\theta
as in (
6.8
) belongs to
U
, and
\bar{\alpha}=\theta\alpha
gives the claim.
∎

We now let

\sigma=\mathcal{R}_{I}\vee\mathscr{L}^{U}=\nabla_{I}\cup\mathscr{L}^{U}{\restriction}_{K}=\big\{{(\alpha,\beta)}:{\alpha,\beta\in I\text{ or }[\alpha,\beta\in K\text{ and }U\alpha=U\beta]\ }\big\}

(6.10)

be the right congruence of
\mathcal{B}_{n}
from (
3.9
). Of course
I
is a
\sigma
-class. By definition, and since
{\mathscr{L}^{U}\subseteq\mathscr{L}(=\mathscr{L}^{\mathcal{B}_{n}})}
, every other
\sigma
-class is contained in
L\cap K
for some
\mathscr{L}
-class
L
of
\mathcal{B}_{n}
. It follows from Lemma
3.8
that every such
\sigma
-class is also an
\mathscr{L}^{U}
-class. The next result tells us how many such classes are contained in
L\cap K
. For the proof, we define a map

\phi:\mathcal{B}_{n}\to\mathcal{S}_{n}\cup\mathcal{S}_{n-2}\cup\mathcal{S}_{n-4}\cup\cdots,

as follows. Let
\alpha\in\mathcal{B}_{n}
, and write
\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)=\{a_{1}<\cdots<a_{r}\}
and
\operatorname{codom}(\alpha)=\{b_{1}<\cdots<b_{r}\}
. Then we define
\phi(\alpha)
to be the permutation
f\in\mathcal{S}_{r}
for which
\alpha
contains the transversal
\{a_{i},b_{if}^{\prime}\}
for each
i
.

Lemma 6.11
.

Let
L
be an
\mathscr{L}
-class of
\mathcal{B}_{n}
, and let the common rank of its elements be
r
. Then

|(L\cap K)/\sigma|=|(L\cap K)/\mathscr{L}^{U}|=r!/|\mathcal{G}_{r}|.

Consequently,

|(D_{r}\cap K)/\sigma|=|(D_{r}\cap K)/\mathscr{L}^{U}|=p_{r}r!/|\mathcal{G}_{r}|.

Proof.

Since
D_{r}
contains
p_{r}

\mathscr{L}
-classes, it suffices to prove the first claim. We do this by showing that

\alpha\mathrel{\sigma}\beta\hskip 20.00003pt\Leftrightarrow\hskip 20.00003pt\phi(\alpha)\phi(\beta)^{-1}\in\mathcal{G}_{r}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $\alpha,\beta\in L\cap K$.}

So let
\alpha,\beta\in L\cap K
, write
f=\phi(\alpha)
and
g=\phi(\beta)
, let
{\operatorname{codom}(\alpha)=\operatorname{codom}(\beta)=\{b_{1}<\cdots<b_{r}\}}
, and let (all) the non-trivial
\operatorname{coker}(\alpha)=\operatorname{coker}(\beta)
-classes be
B_{1},\ldots,B_{s}
. We then have

\bar{\alpha}=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1&\cdots&r&\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil b_{1\!f}&\cdots&b_{r\!f}&B_{1}&\cdots&B_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\and\bar{\beta}=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1&\cdots&r&\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil b_{1g}&\cdots&b_{rg}&B_{1}&\cdots&B_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr).

(6.12)

Since
\alpha,\beta\in K
, it follows from Lemma
6.9
(and the definition of
\sigma
) that

\alpha\mathrel{\sigma}\beta\ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ \alpha\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}^{U}\beta\ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ \bar{\alpha}\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}^{U}\bar{\beta}\ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ \bar{\alpha}\in U\bar{\beta}.

So it remains to show that

\bar{\alpha}\in U\bar{\beta}\hskip 20.00003pt\Leftrightarrow\hskip 20.00003ptfg^{-1}\in\mathcal{G}_{r}.

(6.13)

For the forward implication, suppose
\bar{\alpha}=\theta\bar{\beta}
for some
\theta\in U
. Examining (
6.12
), we see that
\theta
fixes
{\bf r}=\{1,\ldots,r\}
set-wise, and so
\vartheta=\theta{\restriction}_{\bf r}\in\mathcal{S}_{r}
. Moreover, it follows from
\theta\in U=\mathcal{G}_{n}
that
\vartheta\in\mathcal{G}_{r}
. Now, for every
i\in{\bf r}
, the product
\theta\bar{\beta}
contains the transversal
\{i,b_{i\vartheta g}^{\prime}\}
. Keeping in mind that
\theta\bar{\beta}=\bar{\alpha}
, and comparing with (
6.12
), it follows that
f=\vartheta g
, i.e.
fg^{-1}=\vartheta\in\mathcal{G}_{r}
.

Conversely, if
fg^{-1}\in\mathcal{G}_{r}
, then
\bar{\alpha}=\theta\bar{\beta}
, where
\theta\in U
is such that:

i\theta=ifg^{-1}\text{ for $1\leq i\leq r$}\and j\theta=j\text{ for $r+1\leq j\leq n$.}

This completes the proof of (
6.13
), and hence of the lemma.
∎

Remark 6.14
.

Using
|\mathcal{G}_{r}|=2^{s}s!
, where
s=\lfloor r/2\rfloor
, one can show that
r!/|\mathcal{G}_{r}|=r!!
or
(r-1)!!
for
r
odd or even, respectively.

6.3
Upper bound – odd case

We are now ready to construct faithful actions of the degree specified in Theorem
6.1
. Here we consider the case that
n\geq 3
is odd, and the even case will be treated in Section
6.4
. We continue to fix the partition

}}
, and the right congruence
\sigma
from (
6.10
).
Let

\Omega=\big\{{[\alpha]_{\sigma}}:{\alpha\in I_{3}}\big\},\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003ptI_{3}=D_{1}\cup D_{3}.

Since
I_{3}
is an ideal of
\mathcal{B}_{n}
, the action of
\mathcal{B}_{n}
on
\mathcal{B}_{n}/\sigma
restricts to an action

\mu:\Omega\times\mathcal{B}_{n}\to\Omega\hskip 20.00003pt\text{given by}\hskip 20.00003pt\mu([\alpha]_{\sigma},\beta)=[\alpha]_{\sigma}\beta=[\alpha\beta]_{\sigma}.

(6.15)

Theorem 6.16
.

For odd
n\geq 3
, the action
\mu:\Omega\times\mathcal{B}_{n}\to\Omega
is faithful and monogenic, and consequently

\deg(\mathcal{B}_{n})\leq\deg_{\operatorname{rc}}(\mathcal{B}_{n})\leq 1+3p_{3}+p_{1}.

Proof.

As explained in Section
6.1
, the minimal congruences of
\mathcal{B}_{n}
are
(\zeta,\alpha)^{\sharp}
and
(\zeta,\beta)^{\sharp}
, where
\alpha
and
\beta
are as in (
6.5
). Thus, by Lemma
3.4
, we can establish faithfulness by showing that
\mu
separates
(\zeta,\alpha)
and
(\zeta,\beta)
. For this we let

, and we note that

[\pi]_{\sigma}\zeta=[\zeta]_{\sigma},\hskip 20.00003pt[\pi]_{\sigma}\alpha=[\alpha]_{\sigma}=I\and[\pi]_{\sigma}\beta=[\beta]_{\sigma}.

These are distinct because
\zeta,\beta\in K
, and
[\zeta]_{\sigma}=U\zeta=\{\zeta\}
.

For monogenicity, we claim that
\Omega
is generated by
[\pi]_{\sigma}
. For this, we have seen that
I=[\pi]_{\sigma}\alpha
. Any other
\sigma
-class has the form
[\delta]_{\sigma}
for some
\delta\in I_{3}\cap K
, and by Lemma
6.9
we have

[\delta]_{\sigma}=[\bar{\delta}]_{\sigma}=[\pi\bar{\delta}]_{\sigma}=[\pi]_{\sigma}\bar{\delta}.

Finally, using Lemma
6.11
, we have

\deg_{\operatorname{rc}}(\mathcal{B}_{n})\leq|\Omega|=1+|(D_{3}\cap K)/\sigma|+|(D_{1}\cap K)/\sigma|=1+3p_{3}+p_{1}.\qed

, and the right congruence
\sigma
from (

).
Unlike the odd case, minimum-degree faithful actions of
\mathcal{B}_{n}
are not always monogenic; cf. Remark
6.3
. Consequently, we will define such an action by first defining two separate actions
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
, and then taking an appropriate push-out
\mu_{1}\sqcup_{\xi}\mu_{2}
.

In all that follows, an important role will be played by the set

J=I_{2}\cap K=I_{2}\setminus I=\{{\alpha\in\mathcal{B}_{n}}:{\operatorname{rank}(\alpha)\leq 2,\ \ker(\alpha)\subseteq\kappa}\}.

Lemma 6.17
.

J
is a right ideal of
\mathcal{B}_{n}
.

Proof.

Let
\alpha\in J
and
\beta\in\mathcal{B}_{n}
; we must show that
\alpha\beta\in J
, which amounts to showing that
\alpha\beta\in K
, as we certainly have
\alpha\beta\in I_{2}
. Now
\alpha\beta
contains all the upper blocks of
\alpha
, and these are either

Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{k}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{or}\hskip 20.00003ptZ_{1},\ldots,Z_{i-1},Z_{i+1},\ldots,Z_{k}\text{\ \ for some $i\in{\bf k}$.}

In the first case, the upper blocks of
\alpha\beta
are of course
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{k}
. In the second case, the upper blocks of
\alpha\beta
are the same as for
\alpha
if
\operatorname{rank}(\alpha\beta)=2
, or else
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{k}
if
\operatorname{rank}(\alpha\beta)=0
. In all cases, it follows that
\alpha\beta\in K
.
∎

, we also define

{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 35.43 0 C 35.43 2.61 37.55 4.72 40.16 4.72 M 40.16 4.72 L 42.52 4.72 M 42.52 4.72 C 45.13 4.72 47.24 2.61 47.24 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 82.68 0 C 82.68 2.61 84.79 4.72 87.4 4.72 M 87.4 4.72 L 89.76 4.72 M 89.76 4.72 C 92.37 4.72 94.49 2.61 94.49 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{0.4pt,2.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray={0.4pt,2.0pt},stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 53.15 0 L 76.77 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}}
. It follows from the proof of Lemma
6.17
that

\Omega_{1}=R_{\gamma}\cup R_{\zeta}=\{{\alpha\in\mathcal{B}_{n}}:{\alpha\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}\gamma\text{ or }\alpha\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}\zeta}\}

(6.18)

is a right ideal of
\mathcal{B}_{n}
. Consequently, we have an action

\mu_{1}:\Omega_{1}\times\mathcal{B}_{n}\to\Omega_{1}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{given by}\hskip 20.00003pt\mu_{1}(\alpha,\beta)=\alpha\beta.

To define the second action, we need to work a little harder. We first define a map

\mathcal{B}_{n}\to I_{0}=D_{0}:\alpha=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil a_{1}&\cdots&a_{r}&A_{1}&\cdots&A_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil b_{1}&\cdots&b_{r}&B_{1}&\cdots&B_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\mapsto\widehat{\alpha}=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil a_{1},a_{2}&\cdots&a_{r-1},a_{r}&A_{1}&\cdots&A_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil b_{1},b_{2}&\cdots&b_{r-1,}b_{r}&B_{1}&\cdots&B_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr).

In particular, note that
\widehat{\alpha}=\alpha
for
\alpha\in D_{0}
. By
[
23
, Theorem 8.4]
, the relation

\chi=\Delta_{\mathcal{B}_{n}}\cup\{{(\alpha,\beta)\in I_{2}\times I_{2}}:{\widehat{\alpha}=\widehat{\beta}}\}

is a (two-sided) congruence on
\mathcal{B}_{n}
. We now define the relation

\tau=\nabla_{I}\sqcup\chi{\restriction}_{J}\sqcup\mathscr{L}^{U}{\restriction}_{K\setminus J},

and we note that
K\setminus J=K\cap(D_{4}\cup D_{6}\cup\cdots\cup D_{n})
.

Lemma 6.19
.

\tau
is a right congruence on
\mathcal{B}_{n}
.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{B}_{n}=I\sqcup K
and
K=J\sqcup(K\setminus J)
,
\tau
is the union of equivalences on disjoint sets, and is therefore an equivalence itself. For right-compatibility, let
(\alpha,\beta)\in\tau
and
\theta\in\mathcal{B}_{n}
; we must show that
(\alpha\theta,\beta\theta)\in\tau
. This is clear if
(\alpha,\beta)\in\nabla_{I}
.

Next suppose
(\alpha,\beta)\in\chi{\restriction}_{J}
. Since
\chi
is a congruence we have
(\alpha\theta,\beta\theta)\in\chi
, and since
J
is a right ideal we have
\alpha\theta,\beta\theta\in J
. Together these give
(\alpha\theta,\beta\theta)\in\chi{\restriction}_{J}\subseteq\tau
.

Finally suppose
(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathscr{L}^{U}{\restriction}_{K\setminus J}
. Since
\mathscr{L}^{U}{\restriction}_{K\setminus J}\subseteq\mathscr{L}^{U}{\restriction}_{K}\subseteq\sigma
, it follows that
(\alpha\theta,\beta\theta)
belongs to
\sigma
, and hence to either
\nabla_{I}
or
\mathscr{L}^{U}{\restriction}_{K}
. In the first case we are done, so suppose
(\alpha\theta,\beta\theta)\in\mathscr{L}^{U}{\restriction}_{K}
. Since
\mathscr{L}^{U}\subseteq\mathscr{D}
we have
\operatorname{rank}(\alpha\theta)=\operatorname{rank}(\beta\theta)=r
, say.

•

If
r\geq 4
then
(\alpha\theta,\beta\theta)\in\mathscr{L}^{U}{\restriction}_{K\setminus J}\subseteq\tau
.

•

If
r=0
then
\alpha\theta
and
\beta\theta
belong to
D_{0}\cap K=R
, and are
\mathscr{L}^{U}
- and hence
\mathscr{L}
-related. Since
D_{0}
is
\mathscr{H}
-trivial, it follows that
\alpha\theta=\beta\theta
in this case.

•

Finally, if
r=2
, then from
\alpha\theta,\beta\theta\in D_{2}\cap K(\subseteq J)
and
(\alpha\theta,\beta\theta)\in\mathscr{L}^{U}\subseteq\mathscr{L}
, we can write

\alpha\theta=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 2i-1&2i&\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil a&b&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{k-1}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\and\beta\theta=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 2j-1&2j&\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil a&b&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{k-1}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for some $1\leq i,j\leq k$.}

We then have
\widehat{\alpha\theta}=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil a,b&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{k-1}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)=\widehat{\beta\theta}
, so that
(\alpha\theta,\beta\theta)\in\chi{\restriction}_{J}\subseteq\tau
. ∎

Now let

\Omega_{2}=\big\{{[\alpha]_{\tau}}:{\alpha\in I_{4}}\big\},\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003ptI_{4}=D_{0}\cup D_{2}\cup D_{4}.

Since
I_{4}
is an ideal, the action of
\mathcal{B}_{n}
on
\mathcal{B}_{n}/\tau
restricts to an action

\mu_{2}:\Omega_{2}\times\mathcal{B}_{n}\to\Omega_{2}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{given by}\hskip 20.00003pt\mu_{2}([\alpha]_{\tau},\beta)=[\alpha\beta]_{\tau}.

It follows from the definition of
\tau
that

\Omega_{2}=\{I\}\cup(D_{4}\cap K)/\mathscr{L}^{U}\cup\Omega_{3},\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003pt\Omega_{3}=\big\{{[\alpha]_{\tau}}:{\alpha\in J}\big\}=\big\{{[\alpha]_{\tau}}:{\alpha\in R_{\zeta}}\big\},

(6.20)

and that each
\tau
-class from
\Omega_{3}
is in fact a
\chi
-class. Since
\widehat{\alpha}=\alpha
for all
\alpha\in R_{\zeta}
, the map

\xi:R_{\zeta}\to\Omega_{3}:\alpha\mapsto[\alpha]_{\tau}

is a bijection. Since
R_{\zeta}
is a right ideal of
\mathcal{B}_{n}
, it follows that
R_{\zeta}
and
\Omega_{3}
are sub-acts of
\Omega_{1}
and
\Omega_{2}
, respectively, and it is easy to see that
\xi
is in fact an isomorphism of these sub-acts. As in (
3.1
), we can therefore form the push-out

\mu=\mu_{1}\sqcup_{\xi}\mu_{2}:\Omega\times\mathcal{B}_{n}\to\Omega,\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003pt\Omega=\Omega_{1}\sqcup_{\xi}\Omega_{2}.

Theorem 6.21
.

For even
n\geq 4
, the action
\mu:\Omega\times\mathcal{B}_{n}\to\Omega
is faithful, and consequently

\deg(\mathcal{B}_{n})\leq 1+3p_{4}+2p_{2}+p_{0}.

Proof.

As explained in Section
6.1
, the minimal congruences of
\mathcal{B}_{n}
are
(\zeta,\alpha)^{\sharp}
,
(\zeta,\beta)^{\sharp}
and
(\gamma,\delta)^{\sharp}
, where these partitions are as in (
6.6
). Thus, by Lemma
3.4
, we can establish faithfulness by showing that
\mu
separates
(\zeta,\alpha)
,
(\zeta,\beta)
and
(\gamma,\delta)
. Since
\mu=\mu_{1}\sqcup_{\xi}\mu_{2}
, we can do this by showing that
\mu_{1}
separates
(\zeta,\beta)
and
(\gamma,\delta)
, while
\mu_{2}
separates
(\zeta,\alpha)
. For the former we have

\mu_{1}(\zeta,\zeta)=\zeta,\hskip 20.00003pt\mu_{1}(\zeta,\beta)=\beta,\hskip 20.00003pt\mu_{1}(\gamma,\gamma)=\gamma\and\mu_{1}(\gamma,\delta)=\delta,

which are all distinct.
For the latter, and with
\pi={\lower 6.02773pt\hbox{

, we have

\mu_{2}([\pi]_{\tau},\zeta)=[\zeta]_{\tau}\and\mu_{2}([\pi]_{\tau},\alpha)=[\alpha]_{\tau}=I.

It now follows that

\deg(\mathcal{B}_{n})\leq|\Omega|=|\Omega_{1}\sqcup_{\xi}\Omega_{2}|=|\Omega_{1}|+|\Omega_{2}|-|\Omega_{3}|.

Combining (
6.18
) and (
6.20
) with Lemma
6.11
we have

|\Omega_{1}|=|R_{\gamma}|+|R_{\zeta}|=2p_{2}+p_{0}\and|\Omega_{2}|=1+3p_{4}+|\Omega_{3}|.

It follows that
|\Omega|=1+3p_{4}+2p_{2}+p_{0}
.
∎

6.5
Lower bound – odd case

We now turn to the task of showing that the claimed value for
\deg(\mathcal{B}_{n})
in Theorem
6.1
is a lower bound, again treating the odd and even cases in separate sections.

Here we assume
n=2k+1\geq 3
is odd, and we fix
\zeta,\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal{B}_{n}
, as in (
6.5
). For a subset
{X\subseteq P=P(\mathcal{B}_{n})}
we write
\bar{X}=\{{\bar{\varepsilon}}:{\varepsilon\in X}\}
, where here
\bar{\varepsilon}
is as in (
6.7
). We will also use cycle notation for permutations from
\mathcal{S}_{n}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{n}
, so that for example

. We fix the cyclic subgroup

\mathcal{C}_{3}=\langle(1,2,3)\rangle=\{\operatorname{id}_{n},(1,2,3),(1,3,2)\}\leq\mathcal{S}_{n}.

Lemma 6.22
.

Let
\sigma
be a right congruence of
\mathcal{B}_{n}
, where
n\geq 3
is odd.

(i)

If
\sigma
separates
\{\zeta,\alpha\}
, then it separates
\mathcal{C}_{3}\bar{P}_{3}=\{{\xi\bar{\varepsilon}}:{\xi\in\mathcal{C}_{3},\ \varepsilon\in P_{3}}\}
.

(ii)

If
\sigma
separates
\{\zeta,\beta\}
, then it separates
\bar{P}_{1}
.

Proof.

(i)
. As usual, we prove the contrapositive. So suppose
(\xi_{1}\bar{\varepsilon}_{1},\xi_{2}\bar{\varepsilon}_{2})\in\sigma
for some
{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\mathcal{C}_{3}}
and
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\in P_{3}
, with
\xi_{1}\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}\not=\xi_{2}\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}
; we must show that
(\zeta,\alpha)\in\sigma
. Write

\xi_{1}\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1&2&3&\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil a_{1}&a_{2}&a_{3}&A_{1}&\cdots&A_{k-1}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\and\xi_{2}\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1&2&3&\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil b_{1}&b_{2}&b_{3}&B_{1}&\cdots&B_{k-1}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr),

and let
A=\{a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}\}
and
B=\{b_{1},b_{2},b_{3}\}
. We now split into cases; in each we define an element
\theta\in\mathcal{B}_{n}
for which
\{\xi_{1}\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}\theta,\xi_{2}\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}\theta\}=\{\zeta,\alpha\}
.

Case 1.
Suppose first that
A\not=B
. If
a_{1}\not\in\{b_{1},b_{2}\}
or
a_{3}\not\in\{b_{2},b_{3}\}
, then we take
\theta=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil a_{1}&b_{1},b_{2}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1&\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
or
\theta=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil a_{3}&b_{2},b_{3}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1&\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
, respectively. The cases in which
b_{1}\not\in\{a_{1},a_{2}\}
or
b_{3}\not\in\{a_{2},a_{3}\}
are analogous. This leave us to consider the case in which

a_{1}\in\{b_{1},b_{2}\},\hskip 20.00003pta_{3}\in\{b_{2},b_{3}\},\hskip 20.00003ptb_{1}\in\{a_{1},a_{2}\}\and b_{3}\in\{a_{2},a_{3}\}.

Since
A\not=B
, this implies that
a_{2}\not\in B
and
b_{2}\not\in A
, so in fact
a_{1}=b_{1}
and
a_{3}=b_{3}
. We can then assume without loss of generality that
B_{1}=\{a_{2},x\}
for some
x\in{\bf n}
, and we take
\theta=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil x&a_{1},a_{2}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1&\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
.

Case 2.
Next suppose
a_{i}=b_{i}
for
i=1,2,3
. Since
\xi_{1}\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}\not=\xi_{2}\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}
, we can assume without loss of generality that
A_{1}=\{u,v\}
,
B_{1}=\{u,x\}
and
B_{2}=\{v,y\}
for distinct
u,v,x,y\in{\bf n}
. We then take
\theta=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil x&a_{1},u&a_{2},v\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1&\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
.

Case 3.
Up to symmetry, the final case to consider is where
b_{1}=a_{2}
,
b_{2}=a_{3}
and
b_{3}=a_{1}
, and we then take
\theta=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil a_{1}&a_{2},a_{3}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1&\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
.

(ii)
. Suppose
(\bar{\varepsilon}_{1},\bar{\varepsilon}_{2})\in\sigma
for distinct
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\in P_{1}
. Since then
\operatorname{coker}(\varepsilon_{1})\not=\operatorname{coker}(\varepsilon_{2})
, there exist distinct
u,v,w\in{\bf n}
such that
(u,v)\in\operatorname{coker}(\varepsilon_{1})
and
(v,w)\in\operatorname{coker}(\varepsilon_{2})
. Then with
\theta=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil u&v&w\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1&2&3\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
we have
(\beta,\zeta)=(\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}\theta,\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}\theta)\in\sigma
.
∎

We can now complete the proof of Theorem
6.1
in the odd case:

Proposition 6.23
.

If
n\geq 3
is odd, then
\deg(\mathcal{B}_{n})\geq 3p_{3}+p_{1}+1
.

Proof.

Let
\mu:X\times\mathcal{B}_{n}\to X
be a faithful action, denoted
\mu(x,\delta)=x\delta
. We must show that
{|X|\geq 3p_{3}+p_{1}+1}
. Since
\mu
is faithful, and since the minimal congruences of
\mathcal{B}_{n}
are generated by the pairs
(\zeta,\alpha)
and
(\zeta,\beta)
, we can fix elements
x_{1},x_{3}\in X
such that

x_{1}\zeta\not=x_{1}\beta\and x_{3}\zeta\not=x_{3}\alpha.

(6.24)

We define the sets

Y_{1}=x_{1}\bar{P}_{1}=\{{x_{1}\bar{\varepsilon}}:{\varepsilon\in P_{1}}\}\and Y_{3}=x_{3}\mathcal{C}_{3}\bar{P}_{3}=\{{x_{3}\xi\bar{\varepsilon}}:{\xi\in\mathcal{C}_{3},\ \varepsilon\in P_{3}}\},

noting that
|Y_{1}|=p_{1}
and
|Y_{3}|=|\mathcal{C}_{3}\bar{P}_{3}|=3p_{3}
by Lemmas
3.5
and
6.22
. Thus, we can show that
{|X|\geq 3p_{3}+p_{1}+1}
by showing that

Y_{1}\cap Y_{3}=\varnothing\and X\setminus(Y_{1}\cup Y_{3})\not=\varnothing.

(6.25)

To prove the first assertion, suppose to the contrary that
x_{1}\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}=x_{3}\xi\bar{\varepsilon}_{3}
for some
\varepsilon_{i}\in P_{i}
and
\xi\in\mathcal{C}_{3}
. Then with
\theta_{1}=\bar{\varepsilon}_{3}^{*}\xi^{-1}\alpha
and
\theta_{2}=\bar{\varepsilon}_{3}^{*}\xi^{-1}\zeta
, we have

\xi\bar{\varepsilon}_{3}\theta_{1}=\alpha\and\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}\theta_{1}=\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}\theta_{2}=\xi\bar{\varepsilon}_{3}\theta_{2}=\zeta.

It follows that

x_{3}\alpha=(x_{3}\xi\bar{\varepsilon}_{3})\theta_{1}=(x_{1}\bar{\varepsilon}_{1})\theta_{1}=x_{1}\zeta=(x_{1}\bar{\varepsilon}_{1})\theta_{2}=(x_{3}\xi\bar{\varepsilon}_{3})\theta_{2}=x_{3}\zeta,

contradicting (
6.24
).

This leaves us to prove the second assertion in (
6.25
). This is certainly true if
x_{3}\zeta\not\in Y_{1}\cup Y_{3}
, so suppose otherwise.

If
x_{3}\zeta\in Y_{3}
, say with
x_{3}\zeta=x_{3}\xi\bar{\varepsilon}
for
\xi\in\mathcal{C}_{3}
and
\varepsilon\in P_{3}
, then with
\theta=\bar{\varepsilon}^{*}\xi^{-1}\alpha
we have
\zeta\theta=\zeta
and
\xi\bar{\varepsilon}\theta=\alpha
, and this gives
x_{3}\zeta=(x_{3}\zeta)\theta=(x_{3}\xi\bar{\varepsilon})\theta=x_{3}\alpha
, again contradicting (
6.24
).

So we must instead have
x_{3}\zeta\in Y_{1}
, say with
x_{3}\zeta=x_{1}\bar{\varepsilon}
for
\varepsilon\in P_{1}
, and it follows that

x_{3}\zeta=x_{3}\zeta\zeta=x_{1}\bar{\varepsilon}\zeta=x_{1}\zeta.

We complete the proof by showing that
x_{3}\alpha\not\in Y_{1}\cup Y_{3}
. Indeed, if
x_{3}\alpha\in Y_{1}
, say with
x_{3}\alpha=x_{1}\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}
for
\varepsilon_{1}\in P_{1}
, then

x_{3}\alpha=x_{3}\alpha\zeta=x_{1}\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}\zeta=x_{1}\zeta=x_{3}\zeta,

contradicting (
6.24
). But if
x_{3}\alpha\in Y_{3}
, say with
x_{3}\alpha=x_{3}\xi\bar{\varepsilon}_{3}
for
\xi\in\mathcal{C}_{3}
and
\varepsilon_{3}\in P_{3}
, then

x_{3}\alpha=x_{3}\alpha\cdot\bar{\varepsilon}_{3}^{*}\xi^{-1}\zeta=x_{3}\xi\bar{\varepsilon}_{3}\cdot\bar{\varepsilon}_{3}^{*}\xi^{-1}\zeta=x_{3}\zeta,

contradicting (
6.24
).
∎

6.6
Lower bound – even case

We now assume
n=2k\geq 4
is even, and we fix
\zeta,\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\in\mathcal{B}_{n}
, as in (
6.6
). In addition to the subgroup
\mathcal{C}_{3}=\langle(1,2,3)\rangle=\{\operatorname{id}_{n},(1,2,3),(1,3,2)\}
of
\mathcal{S}_{n}
, we also fix
\mathcal{C}_{2}=\langle(1,2)\rangle=\{\operatorname{id}_{n},(1,2)\}
.

Lemma 6.26
.

Let
\sigma
be a right congruence of
\mathcal{B}_{n}
, where
n\geq 4
is even.

(i)

If
\sigma
separates
\{\zeta,\alpha\}
, then it separates
\mathcal{C}_{3}\bar{P}_{4}
.

(ii)

If
\sigma
separates
\{\zeta,\beta\}
, then it separates
\bar{P}_{0}
.

(iii)

If
\sigma
separates
\{\gamma,\delta\}
, then it separates
\mathcal{C}_{2}\bar{P}_{2}
.

Proof.

(i)
. Suppose
(\xi_{1}\bar{\varepsilon}_{1},\xi_{2}\bar{\varepsilon}_{2})\in\sigma
for some
{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\mathcal{C}_{3}}
and
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\in P_{4}
, with
\xi_{1}\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}\not=\xi_{2}\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}
, and write

\xi_{1}\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1&2&3&4&\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil a_{1}&a_{2}&a_{3}&a_{4}&A_{1}&\cdots&A_{k-2}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\and\xi_{2}\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1&2&3&4&\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil b_{1}&b_{2}&b_{3}&b_{4}&B_{1}&\cdots&B_{k-2}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr).

We must show that
(\zeta,\alpha)\in\sigma
. We now consider various cases; in each we define an element
\theta\in\mathcal{B}_{n}
for which
\{\xi_{1}\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}\theta,\xi_{2}\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}\theta\}=\{\zeta,\alpha\}
.

Case 1.
First, if

(a)

\{a_{1},a_{2}\}\cap\{b_{1},b_{4}\}=\varnothing
,

(b)

\{a_{1},a_{2}\}\cap\{b_{2},b_{3}\}=\varnothing
,

(c)

\{a_{3},a_{4}\}\cap\{b_{1},b_{4}\}=\varnothing
, or

(d)

\{a_{3},a_{4}\}\cap\{b_{2},b_{3}\}=\varnothing
,

then we take

(a)

\theta=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil a_{1},a_{2}&b_{1},b_{4}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
,

(b)

\theta=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil a_{1},a_{2}&b_{2},b_{3}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
,

(c)

\theta=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil a_{3},a_{4}&b_{1},b_{4}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
, or

(d)

\theta=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil a_{3},a_{4}&b_{2},b_{3}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
,
respectively.

Case 2.
Now suppose all four of

\{a_{1},a_{2}\}\cap\{b_{1},b_{4}\},\hskip 20.00003pt\{a_{1},a_{2}\}\cap\{b_{2},b_{3}\},\hskip 20.00003pt\{a_{3},a_{4}\}\cap\{b_{1},b_{4}\}\and\{a_{3},a_{4}\}\cap\{b_{2},b_{3}\}

are non-empty, noting then that this forces
\{a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4}\}=\{b_{1},b_{2},b_{3},b_{4}\}
. In particular, this says that
\operatorname{codom}(\varepsilon_{1})=\operatorname{codom}(\varepsilon_{2})
, and it follows that
\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}
and
\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}
have the same transversals. Since
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\mathcal{C}_{3}
, it follows that the ordered tuple
(b_{1},b_{2},b_{3},b_{4})
is equal to either

(a)

(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4})
,

(b)

(a_{2},a_{3},a_{1},a_{4})
, or

(c)

(a_{3},a_{1},a_{2},a_{4})
.

In cases
(b)
and
(c)
we take
\theta=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil a_{1},a_{4}&a_{2},a_{3}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
or
\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil a_{1},a_{2}&a_{3},a_{4}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
, respectively. So, finally consider case
(a)
, in which we have
a_{i}=b_{i}
for
i=1,2,3,4
. Since
\xi_{1}\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}\not=\xi_{2}\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}
, we can assume without loss of generality that
A_{1}=\{u,v\}
,
B_{1}=\{u,x\}
and
B_{2}=\{v,y\}
for distinct
u,v,x,y\in{\bf n}
, and we take
\theta=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil a_{1},u&a_{2},v&a_{3},y&a_{4},x\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
.

(ii)
. Suppose
(\bar{\varepsilon}_{1},\bar{\varepsilon}_{2})\in\sigma
for distinct
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\in P_{0}
, and fix

(u,v)\in\operatorname{coker}(\varepsilon_{1})\and(u,x),(v,y)\in\operatorname{coker}(\varepsilon_{2}),\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003ptu,v,x,y\in{\bf n}\text{ are distinct.}

(6.27)

Then with
\theta=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil x&u&v&y\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1&2&3&4\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
we have
(\beta,\zeta)=(\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}\theta,\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}\theta)\in\sigma
.

(iii)
. Suppose
(\xi_{1}\bar{\varepsilon}_{1},\xi_{2}\bar{\varepsilon}_{2})\in\sigma
for some
{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\mathcal{C}_{2}}
and
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\in P_{2}
, with
\xi_{1}\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}\not=\xi_{2}\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}
, and write

\xi_{1}\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1&2&\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil a_{1}&a_{2}&A_{1}&\cdots&A_{k-1}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\and\xi_{2}\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1&2&\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil b_{1}&b_{2}&B_{1}&\cdots&B_{k-1}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr).

If
a_{1}\not=b_{1}
or
a_{2}\not=b_{2}
, then with
\theta=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil a_{1}&b_{1}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1&2\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
or
\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil a_{2}&b_{2}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 2&1\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
, respectively, we have
{(\gamma,\delta)=(\xi_{1}\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}\theta,\xi_{2}\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}\theta)\in\sigma}
. Now suppose
a_{1}=b_{1}
and
a_{2}=b_{2}
. Since
\xi_{1}\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}\not=\xi_{2}\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}
, we can fix
u,v,x,y\in{\bf n}
as in (
6.27
), and with
\theta=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil v&x&a_{1},u&a_{2},y\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil 1&2&\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
we have
{(\gamma,\delta)=(\xi_{1}\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}\theta,\xi_{2}\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}\theta)\in\sigma}
.
∎

Here then is the final piece of the proof of Theorem
6.1
.

Proposition 6.28
.

If
n\geq 4
is even, then
\deg(\mathcal{B}_{n})\geq 3p_{4}+2p_{2}+p_{0}+1
.

Proof.

Let
\mu:X\times\mathcal{B}_{n}\to X
be a faithful action. We must show that
|X|\geq 3p_{4}+2p_{2}+p_{0}+1
. Since
\mu
is faithful, and since the minimal congruences of
\mathcal{B}_{n}
are generated by the pairs
(\zeta,\alpha)
,
(\zeta,\beta)
and
(\gamma,\delta)
, we can fix elements
x_{0},x_{2},x_{4}\in X
such that

x_{0}\zeta\not=x_{0}\beta,\hskip 20.00003ptx_{2}\gamma\not=x_{2}\delta\and x_{4}\zeta\not=x_{4}\alpha,

(6.29)

where we again use shorthand notation for the action.
We define the sets

Y_{0}=x_{0}\bar{P}_{0},\hskip 20.00003ptY_{2}=x_{2}\mathcal{C}_{2}\bar{P}_{2}\and Y_{4}=x_{4}\mathcal{C}_{3}\bar{P}_{4},

noting that
|Y_{0}|=p_{0}
,
|Y_{2}|=2p_{2}
and
|Y_{4}|=3p_{4}
, by Lemmas
3.5
and
6.26
. Thus, we can show that
{|X|\geq 3p_{4}+2p_{2}+p_{0}+1}
by showing that

\text{$Y_{0}$, $Y_{2}$ and $Y_{4}$ are pairwise disjoint}\and X\setminus(Y_{0}\cup Y_{2}\cup Y_{4})\not=\varnothing.

(6.30)

Before we do this, we first claim that

(Y_{2}\cup Y_{4})\cap\{{x\zeta}:{x\in X}\}=\varnothing.

(6.31)

To prove this, suppose to the contrary that
x\zeta\in Y_{2}\cup Y_{4}
for some
x\in X
.

First consider the case that
x\zeta\in Y_{2}
, and write
x\zeta=x_{2}\xi\bar{\varepsilon}
where
\xi\in\mathcal{C}_{2}
and
\varepsilon\in P_{2}
. Then

x\zeta=x\zeta\bar{\varepsilon}^{*}\xi^{-1}=x_{2}\xi\bar{\varepsilon}\bar{\varepsilon}^{*}\xi^{-1}=x_{2}\gamma,

and we then have
x_{2}\delta=x_{2}\gamma\delta=x\zeta\delta=x\zeta=x_{2}\gamma
, contradicting (
6.29
).

Now suppose instead that
x\zeta\in Y_{4}
, and write
x\zeta=x_{4}\xi\bar{\varepsilon}
where
\xi\in\mathcal{C}_{3}
and
\varepsilon\in P_{4}
. This time we first note that
\zeta\bar{\varepsilon}^{*}\xi^{-1}
is equal to one of

{\lower 6.02773pt\hbox{

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}}
, it follows that

x\zeta\xi_{0}=x\zeta\bar{\varepsilon}^{*}\xi^{-1}=x_{4}\xi\bar{\varepsilon}\bar{\varepsilon}^{*}\xi^{-1}=x_{4}\pi.

But then
x_{4}\zeta=x_{4}\pi\zeta=x\zeta\xi_{0}\zeta=x\zeta=x\zeta\xi_{0}\alpha=x_{4}\pi\alpha=x_{4}\alpha
, contradicting (
6.29
).

Now that we have proved (
6.31
), our next claim is that

Y_{2}\cap Y_{4}=\varnothing.

(6.32)

To prove this, suppose to the contrary that
x_{2}\xi_{2}\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}=x_{4}\xi_{4}\bar{\varepsilon}_{4}
for some
\xi_{2}\in\mathcal{C}_{2}
,
\xi_{4}\in\mathcal{C}_{3}
,
\varepsilon_{2}\in P_{2}
and
\varepsilon_{4}\in P_{4}
. Then

x_{2}\zeta=x_{2}\xi_{2}\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}\bar{\varepsilon}_{4}^{*}\xi_{4}^{-1}\alpha=x_{4}\xi_{4}\bar{\varepsilon}_{4}\bar{\varepsilon}_{4}^{*}\xi_{4}^{-1}\alpha=x_{4}\pi\alpha=x_{4}\alpha.

The previous calculation is valid with
\alpha
replaced by
\zeta
, leading to
x_{2}\zeta=x_{4}\zeta
. Combining these, it follows that
x_{4}\alpha=x_{2}\zeta=x_{4}\zeta
, contradicting (
6.29
).

Now that we have proved (
6.32
), our next claim is that

Y_{0}\cap(Y_{2}\cup Y_{4})=\varnothing.

(6.33)

To prove this, suppose to the contrary that
x_{0}\bar{\varepsilon}_{0}=x_{i}\xi_{i}\bar{\varepsilon}_{i}
, where
i=2
or
4
, and where
\varepsilon_{i}\in P_{i}
and
\xi_{i}\in\mathcal{C}_{2}
or
\mathcal{C}_{3}
as appropriate. We then have
x_{0}\bar{\varepsilon}_{0}\bar{\varepsilon}_{i}^{*}\xi_{i}^{-1}=x_{i}\xi_{i}\bar{\varepsilon}_{i}\bar{\varepsilon}_{i}^{*}\xi_{i}^{-1}
, and we note that

\xi_{i}\bar{\varepsilon}_{i}\bar{\varepsilon}_{i}^{*}\xi_{i}^{-1}=\begin{cases}\gamma&\text{if $i=2$}\\
\pi&\text{if $i=4$,}\end{cases}\and\bar{\varepsilon}_{0}\varepsilon_{i}^{*}\xi_{i}^{-1}=\zeta,\ \beta\text{ or }\beta\delta,

but that
\bar{\varepsilon}_{0}\varepsilon_{i}^{*}\xi_{i}^{-1}=\beta
or
\beta\delta
is only possible when
i=4
. It follows that either

x_{2}\gamma=x_{0}\zeta,\hskip 20.00003ptx_{4}\pi=x_{0}\zeta,\hskip 20.00003ptx_{4}\pi=x_{0}\beta\hskip 20.00003pt\text{or}\hskip 20.00003ptx_{4}\pi=x_{0}\beta\delta.

Keeping in mind that
\gamma=\bar{\gamma}
and
\pi=\bar{\pi}
, the first two options contradict (
6.31
). We now investigate the remaining two cases.
First, if
x_{4}\pi=x_{0}\beta\delta
, then

x_{4}\alpha=x_{4}\pi\alpha=x_{0}\beta\delta\alpha=x_{0}\beta\delta\zeta=x_{4}\pi\zeta=x_{4}\zeta,

contradicting (
6.29
).
So now suppose
x_{4}\pi=x_{0}\beta
. As noted above, this arises when
i=4
(so
{x_{0}\bar{\varepsilon}_{0}=x_{4}\xi_{4}\bar{\varepsilon}_{4}}
) and
\bar{\varepsilon}_{0}\bar{\varepsilon}_{4}^{*}\xi_{4}^{-1}=\beta
. Here we calculate

x_{4}\zeta=x_{4}\pi\zeta=x_{0}\beta\zeta=x_{0}\zeta=x_{0}\bar{\varepsilon}_{0}\bar{\varepsilon}_{0}^{*}=x_{4}\xi_{4}\bar{\varepsilon}_{4}\bar{\varepsilon}_{0}^{*}=x_{4}(\bar{\varepsilon}_{0}\bar{\varepsilon}_{4}^{*}\xi_{4}^{-1})^{*}=x_{4}\beta^{*}=x_{4}\alpha,

again contradicting (
6.29
).

Now that we have proved (
6.32
) and (
6.33
), it remains to prove the second assertion in (
6.30
). This is clearly the case if either
x_{2}\zeta
or
x_{4}\zeta
does not belong to
Y_{0}\cup Y_{2}\cup Y_{4}
. Given (
6.31
), the only other possibility is that
x_{2}\zeta,x_{4}\zeta\in Y_{0}
, and we now assume this is the case. So for
i=2,4
we have
x_{i}\zeta=x_{0}\bar{\varepsilon}_{i}
for some
\varepsilon_{i}\in P_{0}
. It then follows that
x_{i}\zeta=x_{i}\zeta\zeta=x_{0}\bar{\varepsilon}_{i}\zeta=x_{0}\zeta
, so in fact

x_{4}\zeta=x_{2}\zeta=x_{0}\zeta.

We complete the proof by showing that
x_{4}\alpha\not\in Y_{0}\cup Y_{2}\cup Y_{4}
. To do so, suppose to the contrary that
x_{4}\alpha=x_{i}\xi\bar{\varepsilon}
for some
i\in\{0,2,4\}
, and some
\varepsilon\in P_{i}
and
\xi\in\mathcal{C}_{2}\cup\mathcal{C}_{3}
as appropriate. We then note that
\xi\bar{\varepsilon}\bar{\varepsilon}^{*}\xi^{-1}=\zeta
,
\gamma
or
\pi
; in any case it follows that
\xi\bar{\varepsilon}\bar{\varepsilon}^{*}\xi^{-1}\zeta=\zeta
. But then

x_{4}\alpha=x_{4}\alpha\bar{\varepsilon}^{*}\xi^{-1}\zeta=x_{i}\xi\bar{\varepsilon}\bar{\varepsilon}^{*}\xi^{-1}\zeta=x_{i}\zeta=x_{4}\zeta,

contradicting (
6.29
).
∎

7
Combinatorics: the explicit formulae

In Section
6
we obtained an explicit formula for the transformation degree of the Brauer monoid
\mathcal{B}_{n}
; see (
6.2
).
For the other diagram monoids
M=\mathcal{P}_{n}
,
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{n}
,
\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}
,
\mathcal{M}_{n}
and
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
, we showed in Sections
4
and
5
that

\deg(M)=\deg_{\operatorname{rc}}(M)=1+|Q|\and\deg^{\prime}(M)=|Q|

for
n\geq 2
or
3
(as appropriate), where

Q=Q(M)=\begin{cases}P_{0}\cup P_{1}\cup P_{2}&\text{if $M=\mathcal{P}_{n}$, $\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{n}$, $\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}$ or $\mathcal{M}_{n}$}\\
P_{0}\cup P_{2}\cup P_{4}&\text{if $M=\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}$ for even $n$}\\
P_{1}\cup P_{3}&\text{if $M=\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}$ for odd $n$.}\end{cases}

In this final section we obtain explicit formulae for
|Q|
. These are valid for arbitrary
n\geq 0
, but we note that some of the subsets
P_{r}
involved in the above unions are empty for very small
n
.

Formulae for the sizes of
P_{r}=P_{r}(M)=\{{\varepsilon\in P}:{\operatorname{rank}(\varepsilon)=r}\}
are known (see for example
[
13
, Proposition 4.6]
), and of course summing these over appropriate
r
yields formulae for
|Q|
. The resulting expressions are somewhat unwieldy, however. For example, in the case of the partition monoid
\mathcal{P}_{n}
, it follows from
[
13
, Proposition 4.6]
that

|Q|=\sum_{r=0}^{2}\sum_{k=r}^{n}\binom{k}{r}S(n,k)
,
where
S(n,k)
is a Stirling number of the second kind.
Fortunately, since the relevant values of
r
are very small, an alternative combinatorial analysis of these cases is possible, and allows us to derive much simpler expressions for
|Q|
in terms of very fundamental number sequences, such as Bell, Catalan and Motzkin numbers.

Our strategy relies on the existence, for any
0\leq r\leq n
, of an injective mapping

P_{r}(\mathcal{P}_{n})\to P_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{n+r}):\varepsilon=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\mapsto\varepsilon^{+}=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}\cup\{n+r\}&\cdots&A_{r}\cup\{n+1\}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}\cup\{n+r\}&\cdots&A_{r}\cup\{n+1\}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr).

(7.1)

Recall that we always assume
\min(A_{1})<\cdots<\min(A_{r})
when using this tabular notation. It follows that the map
\varepsilon\mapsto\varepsilon^{+}
preserves planarity; it also maps (partial) Brauer diagrams to (partial) Brauer diagrams. As an example, consider a typical projection
\varepsilon\in P_{3}(\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n})
:

\varepsilon\phantom{{}^{+}}=

Here we have only drawn the top half of
\varepsilon
, as the bottom is just the mirror image. We then have

\varepsilon^{+}=

(7.2)

To simplify expressions in what follows, we will write

p_{r}(M)=|P_{r}(M)|\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for any diagram monoid $M$, and any integer $r\geq 0$.}

We therefore have

|Q(M)|=p_{0}(M)+p_{1}(M)+p_{2}(M)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{if $M$ is one of $\mathcal{P}_{n}$, $\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{n}$, $\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n}$ or $\mathcal{M}_{n}$.}

Analogous statements hold for
M=\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
. Our ultimate goal, however, is to express each
|Q(M)|
in terms of the relevant
p_{0}
parameters, which themselves have the following simple forms:

Proposition 7.3

(see
[
13
, Proposition 4.6]
)
.

For
n\geq 0
, we have

(i)

p_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{n})=B(n)
, the
n
th Bell number,

(ii)

p_{0}(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{n})=I(n)
, the
n
th involution number,

(iii)

p_{0}(\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n})=C(n)
, the
n
th Catalan number,

(iv)

p_{0}(\mathcal{M}_{n})=M(n)
, the
n
th Motzkin number,

(v)

p_{0}(\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n})=C(n/2)
for even
n
. ∎

The ubiquity of the numbers occurring in Proposition
7.3
is evidenced by their extremely low sequence numbers as A000085, A000108, A000110 and A001006 on the OEIS
[
1
]
. Note that
I(n)
is the number of involutions (i.e. self-inverse permutations) of
{\bf n}
. These are given by the recurrence

I(0)=I(1)=1\and I(n)=I(n-1)+(n-1)I(n-2)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $n\geq 2$.}

We now proceed to give formulae for
|Q(M)|
, starting with
M=\mathcal{P}_{n}
.

Proposition 7.4
.

For
n\geq 0
we have
\displaystyle|Q(\mathcal{P}_{n})|=\frac{B(n+2)-B(n+1)+B(n)}{2}
.

Proof.

Since
|Q(\mathcal{P}_{n})|=p_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{n})+p_{1}(\mathcal{P}_{n})+p_{2}(\mathcal{P}_{n})
, we can prove the result by showing that:

(i)

p_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{n})=B(n)
,

(ii)

p_{1}(\mathcal{P}_{n})=B(n+1)-B(n)
,

(iii)

p_{2}(\mathcal{P}_{n})=\frac{B(n+2)-3B(n+1)+B(n)}{2}
.

(i)
. This is part
(i)
of Proposition
7.3
.

(ii)
. The image of the map
P_{1}(\mathcal{P}_{n})\to P_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{n+1}):\varepsilon\mapsto\varepsilon^{+}
consists of all projections from
P_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{n+1})
not containing the block
\{n+1\}
. Since there are
p_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{n})
such ‘offending’ projections, we have

p_{1}(\mathcal{P}_{n})=p_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{n+1})-p_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{n})=B(n+1)-B(n).

(iii)
. This time we consider the map
P_{2}(\mathcal{P}_{n})\to P_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{n+2}):\varepsilon\mapsto\varepsilon^{+}
, and calculate the size of its image, via the following steps.

(a)

First we note that
P_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{n+2})
contains
B(n+2)
projections.

(b)

From
B(n+2)
we subtract
B(n+1)
, corresponding to the projections for which
n+1
and
n+2
belong to the same block. (These projections are not in the image of the
\varepsilon\mapsto\varepsilon^{+}
map.)

(c)

We then subtract a further
B(n+1)
, for the projections containing the block
\{n+1\}
.

(d)

We then subtract
B(n+1)
again, for those containing the block
\{n+2\}
.

(e)

We must now add
B(n)
, for the projections containing
both
blocks
\{n+1\}
and
\{n+2\}
.

At this point we are left with a set
\Sigma
of
B(n+2)-3B(n+1)+B(n)
projections, but we must halve this total, as we have double-counted the image of the
\varepsilon\mapsto\varepsilon^{+}
map. Specifically, for

{\varepsilon=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&A_{2}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&A_{2}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\in P_{2}(\mathcal{P}_{n})}
, the set
\Sigma
contains both

\varepsilon^{+}=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}\cup\{n+2\}&A_{2}\cup\{n+1\}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}\cup\{n+2\}&A_{2}\cup\{n+1\}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)\and\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}\cup\{n+1\}&A_{2}\cup\{n+2\}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}\cup\{n+1\}&A_{2}\cup\{n+2\}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr).\qed

Proposition 7.5
.

For
n\geq 0
we have
\displaystyle|Q(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{n})|=\frac{I(n+2)}{2}
.

Proof.

This time we show that:

p_{0}(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{n})=I(n),\hskip 20.00003ptp_{1}(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{n})=I(n+1)-I(n)\and p_{2}(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{n})=\tfrac{I(n+2)-2I(n+1)}{2}.

The first two items are treated identically to the proof of Proposition
7.4
. The third is
almost
identical. We follow steps
(a)
–
(e)
, with
I(k)
in place of
B(k)
, with the only significant difference being in step
(b)
. Here we subtract
I(n)
, rather than
I(n+1)
, as there are
I(n)
projections in
P_{0}(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{n+2})
for which
n+1
and
n+2
belong to the same block (as blocks have size
\leq 2
).
∎

Proposition 7.6
.

For
n\geq 0
we have
\displaystyle|Q(\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n})|=C(n+2)-2C(n+1)+C(n)
.

Proof.

This time the result follows from:

p_{0}(\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n})=C(n),\hskip 10.00002ptp_{1}(\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n})=C(n+1)-C(n)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptp_{2}(\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n})=C(n+2)-3C(n+1)+C(n).

The proof is essentially the same as that of Proposition
7.4
, except that we do not need to divide by
2
in the third calculation, as planarity ensures there is no double-counting.
∎

Proposition 7.7
.

For
n\geq 0
we have
\displaystyle|Q(\mathcal{M}_{n})|=M(n+2)-M(n+1)
.

Proof.

This time we have

p_{0}(\mathcal{M}_{n})=M(n),\hskip 10.00002ptp_{1}(\mathcal{M}_{n})=M(n+1)-M(n)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptp_{2}(\mathcal{M}_{n})=M(n+2)-2M(n+1),

as in Proposition
7.5
, but with no double-counting.
∎

Proposition 7.8
.

For
n\geq 0
we have

\displaystyle|Q(\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n})|=\begin{cases}C(k+1)-C(k)&\text{if $n=2k-1$ is odd}\\
C(k+2)-2C(k+1)+C(k)&\text{if $n=2k$ is even.}\end{cases}

Proof.

Given Proposition
7.6
and the isomorphism
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{2k}\cong\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{k}
, we need only consider the case that
n=2k-1
is odd. Here we have
|Q(\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n})|=p_{1}(\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n})+p_{3}(\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n})
, and we claim that

p_{1}(\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n})=C(k)\and p_{3}(\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n})=C(k+1)-2C(k).

Indeed, the first holds because the map
P_{1}(\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n})\to P_{0}(\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n+1}):\varepsilon\mapsto\varepsilon^{+}
is a bijection. (No Temperley–Lieb partition contains the block
\{n+1\}
.)

For the second, we note that the image of the map
P_{3}(\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n})\to P_{0}(\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n+3}):\varepsilon\mapsto\varepsilon^{+}
consists of all projections from
P_{0}(\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n+3})
not containing the blocks
\{n+1,n+2\}
or
\{n+2,n+3\}
. (See (
7.2
), and note that no Temperley–Lieb partition contains the block
\{n+1,n+3\}
.) We have
p_{0}(\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n+3})=C(k+1)
, and there are
p_{0}(\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n+1})=C(k)
of each of the above two types of ‘offending’ projections, with no overlap between them.
∎

The values of
|Q(M)|
in Propositions
7.4
–
7.8
appear as sequences A000245, A001475, A002026, A026012 and A087649 on the OEIS
[
1
]
.

Remark 7.9
.

Denoting the
forward difference
of a sequence
s(n)
by
{\partial s(n)=s(n+1)-s(n)}
, we see that

|Q(\mathscr{P}\mathcal{P}_{n})|=\partial^{2}C(n),\hskip 10.00002pt|Q(\mathcal{M}_{n})|=\partial M(n+1)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt|Q(\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n})|=\begin{cases}\partial C(k)&\text{for $n=2k-1$}\\
\partial^{2}C(k)&\text{for $n=2k$,}\end{cases}

where
C(n)
and
M(n)
denote Catalan and Motzkin numbers.
